Which Covariates to Adjust for? Specification-robust Causal Inference in Observational Studies
Aditya Ghosh, Dominik Rothenhäusler

Abstract
In observational causal inference, domain knowledge often leaves multiple covariate adjustments plausible, yet which of them satisfy ignorability is not testable. Different adjustments can yield conflicting conclusions about the average treatment effect, and standard remedies (e.g., adjusting for their union or intersection, or reporting the union or convex hull of the confidence intervals) can fail or produce confidence sets that do not shrink to a point with growing sample size. We propose a specification-robust procedure that returns a single point estimate and a confidence interval that is valid when at least one candidate adjustment satisfies ignorability, and has width shrinking at the parametric 1 / n \smash{1/\sqrt{n}} rate. Our approach mirrors how trimming and overlap weighting handle overlap violations: We shift the target to a reweighted population, closest in KL divergence to the original population, for which credible, specification-robust inference is feasible. We also provide diagnostics to assess the population shift and an extension to protect any function of the covariates used for reweighting, similar to adding calipers in matching. Synthetic and real-data examples demonstrate that the proposed procedure provides substantially tighter confidence intervals than the convex hull while achieving nominal coverage for the new target population.



Keywords:

Causal inference, covariate adjustment, ignorability, model misspecification, multiverse analysis, observational studies, reweighted population, robustness.

1
Introduction

In observational studies, covariate adjustment is a standard approach for removing confounding when estimating the average treatment effect (ATE). Valid inference typically relies on two identification conditions: Ignorability (no unmeasured confounding given the chosen covariates) and overlap (positivity). However, domain knowledge often admits several plausible adjustment sets, with little to no empirical basis to choose among them since ignorability is untestable
(
Holland, 1986
;
Pearl, 2009
;
Imbens and Rubin, 2015
)
. Different covariate adjustments can yield conflicting estimates—a recurring theme in multiverse and stability analyses
(
Steegen et al., 2016
;
Yu, 2013
;
Yu and Kumbier, 2020
)
.

A prominent example is the debate on digital media and adolescent well-being: In a large re-analysis of more than 355,000 adolescents,
Orben and Przybylski (2019)
report estimates ranging from harmful to near-zero depending on analytic choices, such as covariate adjustment, exposure and outcome definitions, and missing-data handling. One source of disagreement, for instance, concerns the causal role of the covariates grades and enjoyment of school, whose temporal ordering relative to the exposure is unclear. Different assumptions about their causal role lead to different adjustment sets, which produce conflicting estimates.

When ancestral relationships are known, the disjunctive cause criterion of
VanderWeele and Shpitser (2011)
resolves this ambiguity without requiring the full causal graph. In many applications, however, these ancestral roles can be uncertain, and which adjustment sets satisfy ignorability remains an open question.
Our work addresses this complementary setting of genuine specification uncertainty, where practitioners have identified multiple candidate adjustment sets but cannot determine which lead to valid inference.

Figure 1
:
Illustration of the data-generating mechanisms in
Example

1
(left) and
Example

2
(right). In
Example

1
, both
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are confounders; adjusting only for
\{X_{1}\}
leaves residual confounding from
X_{2}
. In
Example

2
,
X_{2}
is a pre-treatment collider (influenced by the latent variables
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
). Adjusting for
\{X_{1}\}
is valid, but adjusting for
\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
induces
M
-bias by opening the backdoor path
A\leftarrow U_{1}\rightarrow X_{2}\leftarrow U_{2}\rightarrow Y
.

The existing remedies have limitations: Adjusting for the union or intersection of candidate adjustment sets can produce biased estimates (
Table

1
). Averaging estimates, as in
Orben and Przybylski (2019)
, conflates substantive disagreement with the absence of an effect. Another naive approach is to adjust for all the pre-treatment covariates; but this is also known to be problematic in certain settings
(
Ding and Miratrix, 2015
)
; see also
Example

2
, where
X_{2}
is a pre-treatment collider. Recent work by
Yang et al. (2024)
proposes robust testing of a causal null hypothesis when at least one candidate causal model is valid. If one extends their construction to test
H_{0}:\tau=t
and inverts these tests, the resulting confidence set asymptotically retains all the candidate estimands, and hence generally does not shrink to a point when those estimands disagree. Similarly, the convex hull or the union of the confidence intervals maintains nominal coverage when at least one of the candidate adjustments is valid, but its width generally does not vanish as the sample size grows, leaving the ATE partially identified.

When the challenge is overlap rather than ignorability, the field already accepts a pragmatic solution: Redefine the target population to where inference is credible and precise.
For instance, trimming removes units with poor overlap
(
Crump et al., 2009
)
; overlap weighting downweights such units, tilting the estimand toward regions of better comparability

(
Li et al., 2018
)
. This strategy also
applies more broadly: Matching focuses on comparable units
(
Rubin, 1973
;
Rosenbaum and Rubin, 1983b
)
; the local average treatment effect (LATE) restricts attention to compliers
(
Imbens and Angrist, 1994
)
. In all of these cases, researchers accept a modified estimand in exchange for identification and precision. This approach of modifying the estimand, while popular in empirical practice, requires care in transparent reporting of the new target population and interpreting the modified estimand for that population
(
Rizk et al., 2025
)
.

We apply the same principle to handle uncertainty in regression adjustment. Point identification on the original population requires resolving which adjustment set satisfies ignorability, which is often impossible. We shift the target to a reweighted population as close as possible to the original population (in KL-divergence) for which all candidate estimands agree; see
Figure

2
for an illustration. This reweighting, driven entirely by the measured covariates, makes the population shift transparent and enables valid, precise inference.

Figure 2
:
Distribution of the covariates age, education and income for the original population versus the new target population for the 401(k) dataset; see
Section

6.2
for details. We shift the target to a reweighted population chosen as close as possible to the original population such that all candidate covariate adjustments identify the ATE of the reweighted population, provided at least one of them satisfies ignorability.

Our contributions

•

We develop a specification-robust procedure that returns a single point estimate and a confidence interval that is asymptotically valid as long as at least one candidate adjustment set satisfies ignorability, without requiring the knowledge of which one(s).

•

The width of our confidence interval vanishes at the parametric
n^{-1/2}
rate, unlike the convex hull interval whose width remains of constant order.

•

We propose an extension to protect functions of the covariates used for reweighting, similar to adding calipers in matching
(
Rosenbaum and Rubin, 1985
)
. This ensures that the reweighting preserves key features of the original population deemed important by the researcher.

Additionally, we recommend diagnostic plots such as
Figure

2
that compare the reweighted population with the original in terms of the distribution of the covariates, to assess whether the new target population is of scientific interest. In an empirical application studying the effect of 401(k) availability on net financial assets (
Section

6.2
), our proposed method produces a confidence interval
\sim 77\%
narrower than the convex hull interval (
Figure

6
), and targets the ATE of the reweighted population illustrated in
Figure

2
. The accompanying R package and replication files are available at:
https://github.com/ghoshadi/specrobust
.

1.1
Related work

Multiverse analysis and stability

Multiverse analysis
(
Steegen et al., 2016
)
and stability analysis
(
Yu, 2013
;
Yu and Kumbier, 2020
)
both address sensitivity to analytic choices—the former by reporting all plausible analyses, the latter by assessing whether conclusions are robust to appropriate perturbations. These principles have been influential across Bayesian statistics
(
Box, 1980
;
Skene et al., 1986
)
, econometrics
(
Leamer, 1983
)
, and causal inference
(
LaLonde, 1986
;
Rosenbaum, 1987
;
Freedman, 1991
;
Rosenbaum, 2010
;
Imbens and Rubin, 2015
;
Karmakar et al., 2019
)
. We address a similar multiplicity problem, but offer a different solution: Rather than reporting the range of estimates across plausible adjustment sets, we collapse it into a single credible estimate.
Kang et al. (2016)
study a parallel problem for instrumental variables—identifying the causal effect when a majority of candidate instruments are valid.

Causal graphs

There are systematic approaches for selecting valid adjustment sets when the causal graph is known. For example, Pearl’s backdoor criterion
(
Pearl, 2009
)
is a sufficient condition, which
Shpitser et al. (2010)
generalize to a wider class.
VanderWeele and Shpitser (2011)
introduce the disjunctive cause criterion, which selects adjustment sets from ancestral information without requiring the complete graph structure.
Guo et al. (2023b)
provide an overview of such criteria, and
Guo et al. (2023a)
study efficiency when multiple valid sets are available. Another approach is to learn the graph via causal discovery and conduct post-selection inference
(
Chang et al., 2025
)
. These tools can help guide adjustment choices, but uncertainty can remain when multiple plausible graphs are consistent with available knowledge.
Bhattacharya et al. (2026)
consider settings where additional causal assumptions yield testable restrictions. When at least one valid candidate satisfies these restrictions and the invalid ones do not, they propose using a weighted combination of the candidate estimators.
Our work addresses the complementary case where specification uncertainty remains unresolved either by testable implications or by domain knowledge. Given a set of candidate adjustments—whether from graphical reasoning, causal discovery, or prior literature—our method aims to provide valid inference for a reweighted population when at least one candidate adjustment set is valid, without requiring us to identify which one(s).

Sensitivity analysis

Sensitivity analysis methods assess the robustness of causal estimates to ignorability violations, typically through parameters that quantify the degree of unmeasured confounding. A variety of such methods have been introduced, including
Cornfield et al. (1959)
;
Rosenbaum and Rubin (1983a)
;
Robins (1999)
;
Frank (2000)
;
Rosenbaum (2002)
;
Imbens (2003)
;
Brumback et al. (2004)
;
Imai et al. (2010)
;
Hosman et al. (2010)
;
VanderWeele and Arah (2011)
;
Blackwell (2014)
;
Dorie et al. (2016)
;
Cinelli and Hazlett (2020)
;
Oster (2019)
;
Zhao et al. (2019)
;
Franks et al. (2020)
;
Dorn and Guo (2023)
;
Kang et al. (2024)
;
Dorn et al. (2025)
, among others. All of these methods take one adjustment set as given and probe how far ignorability can be violated before conclusions change. We address a different challenge: Rather than perturbing a single specification, we tackle uncertainty about which adjustment set is valid in the first place.

Reweighting methods

Reweighting methods have been a cornerstone of causal inference, from inverse probability weighting
(
Horvitz and Thompson, 1952
)
and its stabilizations via outcome modeling
(
Robins et al., 1994
)
, calibration
(
Deville and Särndal, 1992
)
and entropy balancing
(
Hainmueller, 2012
)
, to overlap weighting
(
Li et al., 2018
)
and weighted instrumental variable methods
(
Small et al., 2017
;
Rakshit et al., 2024
)
. While these methods reweight to improve estimation under a single, assumed-valid adjustment set, our approach reweights to reconcile estimation across multiple adjustment sets, assuming at least one is valid.

2
Point identification via population reweighting

2.1
Problem setup and notation

Suppose that we observe data
D_{i}=(Y_{i},\,A_{i},\,X_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,n
, where
Y_{i}
is the response,
A_{i}\in\{0,1\}
is the binary treatment indicator, and
X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
are the covariates for the
i
-th unit. Denote by
Y_{i}(0)
and
Y_{i}(1)
the potential outcomes under control and treatment, respectively
(
Splawa-Neyman, 1990
;
Rubin, 1974
;
Rubin, 1977
;
Imbens and Rubin, 2015
)
, and assume that SUTVA holds, so that
Y_{i}=Y_{i}(A_{i})
.

Assumption 1
.

\{(Y_{i}(0),\,Y_{i}(1),\,A_{i},\,X_{i})\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
are independent observations from an unknown distribution
P
. Denote by
(Y(0),\,Y(1),\,A,\,X)
a generic observation from
P
.

Our parameter of interest is the average treatment effect (ATE) defined as

\overline{\tau}:=\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y(1)-Y(0)].

For any subset
S\subseteq\{1,2,\dots,p\}
we use
X_{S}
to denote the covariates
\{X_{s}:s\in S\}
.

Definition 2.1
.

Call an adjustment set
S

valid
(under
P
) if it satisfies the ignorability condition: The treatment is independent of the potential outcomes when we condition on the covariates in that set, i.e.,

{A\perp\!\!\!\!\perp Y(a)\,\mid\,X_{S},\quad a=0,1}.

We say that an adjustment set
S
is
invalid
if it does not satisfy the above.

An invalid adjustment set can compromise inference for two broad reasons. First, it can leave out an important confounder (
Example

1
). Second, including certain variables can induce conditional dependence between the treatment and potential outcomes (
Example

2
). Given a causal graph,
Shpitser et al. (2010)
provide
a necessary and sufficient graphical criterion for the validity of a covariate adjustment; see
Pearl (2009)
for a book-level treatment of graphical causal inference.
Our focus is on settings where such a specification is either unavailable or contested. Concretely, suppose that we have
K
candidate adjustment sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{K}
that satisfy the following condition.

Assumption 2
.

At least one of the candidate adjustment sets is valid (we do not know which one(s)), and their intersection
S_{\cap}
is non-empty. Assume also that each adjustment set
S_{k}
satisfies strong overlap:
\xi\leq P(A=1\mid X_{S_{k}})\leq 1-\xi
almost surely, for some
0<\xi<1
.

Even when ignorability is violated, many off-the-shelf CATE estimators can still be used to estimate the conditional mean contrasts:

\tau(X_{S_{k}}):=\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\mid A=1,X_{S_{k}}]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\mid A=0,X_{S_{k}}].

(1)

When the adjustment set
S_{k}
is valid, the regression contrast
\smash{\tau(X_{S_{k}})}
coincides with the conditional average treatment effect (CATE) and its expectation coincides with the ATE
\overline{\tau}
. However, for an invalid adjustment set
S_{k}
, the regression contrast
\smash{\tau(X_{S_{k}})}
does not have a causal interpretation, and its expectation can differ arbitrarily from
\overline{\tau}
.

As discussed in
Section

1
, the naive approach of reporting the convex hull of the confidence intervals constructed using all candidate adjustment sets provides a valid confidence interval for
\overline{\tau}
under
Assumptions

1
and
2
. However, even when the sample size grows to infinity, this interval shrinks to the convex hull of the points
\{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})]\}_{k=1}^{K}
which is of constant order.
Indeed, the specification uncertainty not resolved by the observed data law leaves the ATE only partially identified whenever the candidate estimands disagree; see
Lemma

4.2
for a formal statement.

2.2
Our specification-robust approach

Figure 3
:
Distributions of the covariate
X_{1}
under the original population (
P
) and the new target population (
w^{*}P
) for
Example

1
(left) and
Example

2
(right). We find the reweighted population as close as possible to the original population such that all the candidate adjustments identify the ATE of the population
w^{*}P
. In this example,
w^{*}
is only a function of
X_{1}
, and
X_{2}
, being independent of
X_{1}
, retains the same distribution under the reweighting.

We shift the target to a new population by reweighting the covariates while keeping the conditional distribution of potential outcomes and treatment unchanged, i.e., consider reweighted populations of the form

wP=wP_{X}\cdot P_{Y(1),\,Y(0),\,A\,\mid\,X},\quad w\geq 0,\quad\mathbb{E}_{P}[w]=1.

(2)

Here
w(\cdot)
denotes the likelihood ratio of the new target population with respect to the original.
Our strategy is to find the population closest in Kullback--Leibler (KL) divergence
1
1

1

All twice continuously differentiable
f
-divergences share the same second-order Taylor expansion around unity (up to normalization), so for small perturbations the choice of divergence is inconsequential. We use the KL divergence since it yields tractable weight expressions that facilitate the bias-correction needed for parametric rate (
Section

3
).
to the original population for which all candidate adjustment sets target the same estimand. Formally, consider the optimization problem:

\begin{split}w^{*}=&\mathop{\rm argmin}_{w=w(X_{S_{\cap}})\geq 0,\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[w]=1}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(wP\,\|\,P)\\
&\text{subject to}\ \ \mathbb{E}_{P}[w(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau(X_{S_{1}})]=\cdots=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau(X_{S_{K}})].\end{split}

(3)

Throughout, we restrict to weights
w
satisfying
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w(X_{S_{\cap}})|\tau(X_{S_{k}})|]<\infty
for every
k
, so that the constraints in (
3
) are well defined. Two key design choices in (
3
) are that we restrict to weights that only depend on the covariates shared by all candidate adjustment sets, and we match the mean of the contrasts exactly on the reweighted population. We motivate these in
Section

4.1
.
By construction, all candidate adjustment sets target a common estimand for the reweighted population:

\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}:=\mathbb{E}_{w^{*}P}[\tau(X_{S_{1}})]=\mathbb{E}_{w^{*}P}[\tau(X_{S_{2}})]=\cdots=\mathbb{E}_{w^{*}P}[\tau(X_{S_{K}})].

(4)

Unlike the original contrasts
\tau(X_{S_{k}})
lacking a causal interpretation when
S_{k}
is not valid, the
new target parameter
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}
defined in (
4
) has a causal interpretation under
Assumption

2
:

Lemma 2.2

(Interpretation of the reweighted estimand)
.

Suppose that the adjustment sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{K}
satisfy
Assumption

2
and
w^{*}
solves the optimization problem (
3
). Then, for each
k=1,2,\dots,K
,

\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau(X_{S_{k}})]=\mathbb{E}_{w^{*}P}[Y(1)-Y(0)].

Lemma

2.2
emphasizes that by solving (
3
) we not only find a reweighted population close to the original population for which all candidate adjustments mechanically target the same estimand, but the common estimand also coincides with the ATE of the new target population.
We refer the reader to
Figures

2
and
3
for a visual comparison of the reweighted population and the original population for our empirical examples; see
Section

6
for details.

In the following section, we develop the supporting theory, from solving (
3
) empirically to constructing specification-robust confidence intervals using debiased machine learning. We relegate additional technical results to
Section

4
, and further elaborate on the interpretation and practical usefulness of the reweighted population for real-world applications in
Section

5
.

3
Theory for estimation and inference

Given candidate adjustment sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{K}
satisfying
Assumption

2
, our goal is to estimate and draw inference on the ATE
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}
of the reweighted population introduced in
Section

2.2
. We proceed in two steps. First, in
Section

3.1
, we characterize the solution to the population-level problem (
3
) and use this to lay out the construction of empirical transfer weights.
Then, in
Section

3.2
, we construct an estimator of
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}

that aggregates the augmented inverse probability weighting (AIPW) estimators based on each adjustment set, together with an additional bias-correction to account for the uncertainty in estimating the transfer weights. The proposed estimator is asymptotically normal at the
n^{-1/2}
rate (
Theorem

3.2
), using which we obtain specification-robust confidence intervals (
Corollary

3.4
).

3.1
Transfer weights via exponential tilting

Solving the optimization problem (
3
) might initially appear as challenging since it involves optimization over an infinite-dimensional space of reweighting functions. However, recognizing (
3
) as an I-projection onto a moment-constrained set, we can apply classical results from information theory
(
Csiszár, 1975
;
Cover and Thomas, 2005
)
to reduce it to a finite dimensional convex optimization problem via exponential tilting. We state the relevant result tailored to our setting in
Lemma

3.1
, which relies on the following sufficient conditions for the existence and uniqueness of a solution.

Assumption 3
.

Define differences-in-contrasts
2
2

2

The choice of
1
as the reference index does not affect the reweighted population or the resulting estimand, since the constraint set
\{\mathbb{E}_{wP}[\tau(X_{S_{\ell}})-\tau(X_{S_{k}})]=0,\,1\leq k\leq K\}
spans the same affine subspace regardless of which index
\ell
is designated as the reference.

\Delta\tau_{k}(X):=\tau(X_{S_{1}})-\tau(X_{S_{k}})
where
\tau(X_{S_{k}})
is defined in (
1
). Write
\Delta\tau:=(\Delta\tau_{2},\dots,\Delta\tau_{K})^{\top}
, and define

g(X_{S_{\cap}}):=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\Delta\tau(X)\mid X_{S_{\cap}}],\qquad\psi_{P}(\lambda):=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\exp(g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\lambda)]\,\in(0,\infty].

(5)

Assume that
(i)
3
3

3

In fact, condition (i) is sufficient as well as necessary for the moment generating function
\psi_{P}(\cdot)
to have a finite and unique minimizer; see
Section

C.2
for a proof.
there is no
v\neq 0
such that
v^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}})\leq 0
almost surely under
P
, and (ii) the infimum of
\lambda\mapsto\psi_{P}(\lambda)
is not attained on the boundary of
\{\lambda:\psi_{P}(\lambda)<\infty\}
.

We note that
Assumption

3
is essentially a heterogeneity assumption on how the contrasts vary with the shared covariates. In particular, it rules out cases where regardless of how we reweight based on the shared covariates, the contrasts cannot be matched as in (
3
) (e.g., when one or more of the differences-in-contrasts are always positive or always negative). We refer the reader to
Section

5.3
for the practical implications of infeasibility and possible remedies. The following result states how
Assumption

3
implies the existence and uniqueness of the solution to (
3
) and also reduces it to a finite-dimensional convex optimization problem.

Lemma 3.1

(Existence and uniqueness of transfer weights)
.

Under
Assumption

3
(i), the moment generating function
\psi_{P}(\cdot)
defined in (
5
) has a unique, finite minimizer
\lambda^{*}
, i.e.,

\lambda^{*}:=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}}\ \psi_{P}(\lambda),

(6)

If, in addition,
Assumption

3
(ii) holds, then (
3
) has a unique (
P
-a.s.) solution given by the exponential tilt:

w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})={\exp\big(g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\lambda^{*}\big)}\;/\;{\psi_{P}(\lambda^{*})}.

Estimating the transfer weights

In light of
Lemma

3.1
, we can empirically solve (
3
) using numerical convex optimization (see
Boyd and Vandenberghe (2004)
for a book-level treatment). Throughout, we use
\mathbb{P}_{n}[\psi(D)]
to denote
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(D_{i})
, for any measurable function
\psi
of a generic observation
D=(Y,A,X)
. We perform the following steps:

1.

Use any off-the-shelf algorithm that estimates the regression contrasts
\tau(X_{S_{k}})
regardless of whether ignorability holds (e.g., generalized random forests;
Wager and Athey, 2018
;
Athey et al., 2019
).

2.

Regress
\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})
further on the shared covariates
X_{S_{\cap}}
and take their difference to obtain an estimator
\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})
of the function
g(X_{S_{\cap}})
defined in (
5
).

3.

Use any standard convex optimization routine to numerically solve the following problem:

\widehat{\lambda}:=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}}\;\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\exp\left(\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\lambda\right)\right]=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}}\;\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp\left(\widehat{g}(X_{i,S_{\cap}})^{\top}\lambda\right).

(7)

4.

Obtain transfer weights for each unit as

\widehat{w}(X_{i,S_{\cap}}):={\exp\big(\widehat{g}(X_{i,{S_{\cap}}})^{\top}\,\widehat{\lambda}\big)}\;\big/\;{\mathbb{P}_{n}\big[\exp\big(\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\,\widehat{\lambda}\big)\big]}.

For ease of exposition, we described above the main steps for estimating the transfer weights using the full sample. In practice, however, it is not desirable to evaluate the weight function on the same observations used to estimate it, since it can lead to overfitting. To avoid this, we split the sample into
L\geq 2
folds and use cross-fitting (see, e.g.,
Chernozhukov et al. (2018)
);
Algorithm

1
gives a complete description of our procedure. In the following section, we turn to estimation and inference for the ATE of the reweighted population.

3.2
Specification-robust estimation and inference

With the transfer weights
\widehat{w}(X_{i,S_{\cap}})
estimated as in
Section

3.1
, we can estimate the ATE
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}
of the reweighted population using any of the following estimators:

\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}:=\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})\right],\quad k=1,\dots,K.

(8)

Since all of these estimators
\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}_{k}
target the same estimand
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}
, we can aggregate them via an affine combination and report a single point estimate:

\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}(\nu)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\mathtt{R}},\quad\text{where}\quad\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}=1.

(9)

In the special case where the baseline estimators
\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})
are based on linear regressions with treatment-covariate interactions (at least one correctly specified), the above estimator achieves the fast
\sqrt{n}
-rate of convergence for any choice of the affine weights
\nu
. We analyze this special case in
Appendix

A
.

More generally, modern machine learning methods offer more flexible ways to estimate the conditional contrasts.
The literature on semiparametric inference and double/debiased machine learning
(
Robins et al., 1994
;
Robins and Rotnitzky, 1995
;
Hahn, 1998
;
Scharfstein et al., 1999
;
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
;
Chernozhukov et al., 2021
)
provides a general framework for using such flexible estimators, together with augmented inverse probability weighting (AIPW) that eliminates first-order sensitivity to nuisance estimation errors.
With this motivation, we replace the generic nonparametric estimators
\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})
in (
8
) with their AIPW versions.
The resulting reweighted estimators, however, still inherit a first-order error from estimating the transfer weights. This has two components, namely from estimating the function
g
defined in (
5
) and the exponential tilt parameter
\lambda^{*}
defined in (
6
). We handle these sources of nuisance error as follows. First, we carefully choose the affine weights
\nu
in (
9
) that mitigate the first-order error from estimating
\lambda^{*}
. Then, we handle the remaining first-order error by adding a bias-correction term.
Algorithm

1
provides a more explicit description of this debiasing procedure, and
Proposition

4.3
provides further insights through Neyman orthogonality.

The following result states the asymptotic distribution of the bias-corrected estimator.
To keep the exposition simple, we assume here that the nonparametric estimators taken as input in
Algorithm

1
are all fit externally, i.e., independent of the data they are evaluated on. In our software implementation we split the sample into
L\geq 2
folds and use cross-fitting.
A standard conditioning argument then implies that the same asymptotic distribution holds for the cross-fitted estimator computed in
Algorithm

1
.

Theorem 3.2

(Asymptotic normality of the specification-robust estimator)
.

Assume that the propensity score estimators
\widehat{e}(X_{S_{k}})
, conditional response estimators
\widehat{\mu}_{a}(X_{S_{k}})
and nonparametric regressors
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P}[\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
used in
Algorithm

1
are fit externally, i.e., independent of
(Y_{i},A_{i},X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, and that
Assumptions

1
,
2
and
3
hold true. Assume further that,

(i)

(rates of nonparametric regressors) for some exponent
q>2
and for every
1\leq k\leq K
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mu}_{a}(X_{S_{k}})-\mu_{a}(X_{S_{k}})\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}=o_{p}(n^{-\alpha_{\mu}}),\quad\|\widehat{e}\,(X_{S_{k}})-e(X_{S_{k}})\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}=o_{p}(n^{-\alpha_{e}}),

where
\alpha_{\mu}\geq 0
,
\alpha_{e}\geq 0
and
\alpha_{\mu}+\alpha_{e}\geq{1}/{2}
, i.e., the product of the above is
o_{p}(n^{-1/2})
. Moreover,

\|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P}[\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})\,|\,X_{S_{\cap}}]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\,|\,X_{S_{\cap}}]\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}=o_{p}(n^{-1/4}).

(10)

(ii)

(strong overlap) The estimated propensity scores
\widehat{e}\,(X_{S_{k}})
are bounded between
[\xi,1-\xi]
for some
0<\xi<1
.

(iii)

(bounded moments) The moment generating functions of
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
and
g(X_{S_{\cap}})
are finite in a radius
r>\frac{2q}{q-2}\|\lambda^{*}\|
around the origin, and the same holds for
\widehat{g}
conditionally:
\smash{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\exp(r\|\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})\|)\mid\widehat{g}(\cdot)]=O_{p}(1)}
. Also,
{\rm Var}_{P}[Y\mid A=a,X_{S_{\cap}}]\leq C
and
{\rm Var}_{P}[Y\mid A=a,X_{S_{k}}]\leq C
for some constant
C\in(0,\infty)
.

Then, the specification-robust estimator
\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}
we compute in
Algorithm

1
satisfies

\sqrt{n}\left(\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}}).

An explicit expression for the asymptotic variance
\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}}
is deferred to
Section

4.2
.

Algorithm 1

Bias-corrected specification-robust estimator (with cross-fitting)

1:

Data
(Y_{i},A_{i},X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
;
K\geq 2
adjustment sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{K}
with non-empty intersection
S_{\cap}
; black-box machine learning algorithms to estimate the propensity scores
\smash{e(X_{S_{k}})=P(A=1\mid X_{S_{k}})}
, conditional response functions
\smash{\mu_{a}(X_{S_{k}})={\mathbb{E}}_{P}\left[Y\mid A=a,X_{S_{k}}\right]}
, for
a=0,1
,
k=1,\dots,K
, and a regression algorithm for
\smash{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\,\cdot\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]}
; number of folds
L\geq 2
for cross-fitting.

2:

Estimated transfer weights (out-of-fold) and the proposed specification-robust estimator
\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}
for the ATE of the reweighted population.

3:

Split
[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}
into
L
disjoint, nearly equal-sized folds
I_{1},\dots,I_{L}
, and write
I_{-\ell}:=[n]\setminus I_{\ell}
.

4:

for

\ell=1,\dots,L

do

5:

Using data from
I_{-\ell}
, train estimators
\widehat{\mu}_{a}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{k}})
of
\mu_{a}(X_{S_{k}})
and
\widehat{e}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{k}})
of
e(X_{S_{k}})
, for
a=0,1
and
k=1,\dots,K
. Define the AIPW functional

\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k,\,-\ell}(Y,A,X):=\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{k}})-\widehat{\mu}_{0}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{k}})+\frac{A}{\widehat{e}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{k}})}\big(Y-\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{k}})\big)-\frac{1-A}{1-\widehat{e}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{k}})}\big(Y-\widehat{\mu}_{0}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{k}})\big).

6:

Using data from
I_{-\ell}
, regress the out-of-bag fitted (or, internally cross-fitted) values of
\smash{\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{k}})}
and
\smash{\widehat{\mu}_{0}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{k}})}
on
X_{S_{\cap}}
, and take the difference of the two fits to obtain
\widehat{m}_{k}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{\cap}})
estimating the conditional contrast
{m_{k}(X_{S_{\cap}})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]}
.

7:

With
\smash{\widehat{g}_{k}^{-\ell}:=\widehat{m}_{1}^{-\ell}-\widehat{m}_{k}^{-\ell}}
for each
k=2,\dots,K
, set
\smash{\widehat{g}^{-\ell}:=(\widehat{g}_{2}^{-\ell},\dots,\widehat{g}_{K}^{-\ell})^{\top}}
.

8:

Use numerical convex optimization to solve the problem (
7
) on
I_{-\ell}
, with
\widehat{g}^{-\ell}(X_{i,S_{\cap}})
given by out-of-bag (or, internally cross-fitted) predictions for
i\in I_{-\ell}
, and obtain

\widehat{\lambda}^{-\ell}=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}}\frac{1}{|I_{-\ell}|}\sum_{i\in I_{-\ell}}\exp\left(\lambda^{\top}\widehat{g}^{-\ell}(X_{i,S_{\cap}})\right).

9:

Denoting by
\mathbb{P}_{n,\ell}
the empirical mean over
I_{\ell}
, compute transfer weights

\widehat{w}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{\cap}})=\exp\big(\widehat{g}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\widehat{\lambda}^{-\ell}\big)\;\big/\;\mathbb{P}_{n,\ell}\big[\exp\big(\widehat{g}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\widehat{\lambda}^{-\ell}\big)\big],

normalized on
I_{\ell}
so that
\mathbb{P}_{n,\ell}\,[\widehat{w}^{-\ell}]=1
.

10:

Evaluate the nuisance estimators and the weighting function (all learned on
I_{-\ell}
) on
I_{\ell}
to compute the reweighted (AIPW) estimators

\widehat{\tau}_{k,\,\ell}^{\,\mathtt{R}}:=\mathbb{P}_{n,\ell}\left[\widehat{w}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k,\,-\ell}(Y,A,X)\right],\hskip 18.49988ptk=1,\dots,K.

11:

Compute affine weights
\widehat{\nu}_{\ell}
as follows. Set
\widehat{\nu}_{1,\,\ell}:=1-\sum_{k=2}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k,\,\ell}
and

\widehat{\nu}_{2:K,\,\ell}:=\left(\mathbb{P}_{n,\ell}\left[\widehat{w}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{\cap}})\widehat{g}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{\cap}})\widehat{g}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\right]\right)^{-1}\mathbb{P}_{n,\ell}\left[\widehat{w}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{\cap}})\widehat{g}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\widehat{m}_{1}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{\cap}})-\widehat{\tau}_{1,\,\ell}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right].

12:

With
\Delta\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k,\,-\ell}(Y,A,X):=\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{1,\,-\ell}(Y,A,X)-\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k,\,-\ell}(Y,A,X)
, compute a bias-correction term:

\widehat{B}_{\ell}:=\mathbb{P}_{n,\ell}\left[\widehat{w}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\Delta\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{2:K,\,-\ell}(Y,A,X)-\widehat{g}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)^{\!\top}\widehat{\lambda}^{-\ell}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k,\,\ell}\,\widehat{m}_{k}^{-\ell}(X_{S_{\cap}})-\widehat{\tau}_{1,\,\ell}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right].

13:

Aggregate over the folds to compute the bias-corrected specification-robust estimator as

\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{|I_{\ell}|}{n}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k,\,\ell}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k,\,\ell}^{\mathtt{R}}+\widehat{B}_{\ell}\right).

Remark 3.3
.

For the outcome regressions and propensity score estimators,
Theorem

3.2
only requires consistency and the usual product-rate condition. However, for the iterated regressions (the regression contrasts further regressed on the shared covariates), we impose the fourth-root rate as in (
10
), because the remainder in the first-order Taylor expansion of the transfer weights is quadratic in the estimation error of those iterated regressions.
Fourth-root conditions of this form are common in the semiparametric inference literature when the relevant remainder is quadratic in the nuisance estimation error; see, e.g.,
Newey (1994)
;
Chen et al. (2003)
;
Chernozhukov et al. (2018)
;
Nie and Wager (2021)
.

Inference

For any consistent estimator
\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{V}}_{\mathtt{SR}}}
of the asymptotic variance
\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}}
in
Theorem

3.2
, we can construct an asymptotically valid
(1-\alpha)
-confidence interval for
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}
as follows:

\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}:=\left[\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}-\frac{z_{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{V}}_{\mathtt{SR}}},\ \widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}+\frac{z_{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{V}}_{\mathtt{SR}}}\,\right],

(11)

where
z_{\alpha}
is the
(1-\alpha)
-th quantile of standard normal distribution. We propose a plug-in estimator in
Section

B.2.2

and empirically demonstrate the resulting confidence interval in
Section

6.2
.

Corollary 3.4

(Specification-robust confidence intervals)
.

For any consistent estimator
\widehat{\mathrm{V}}_{\mathtt{SR}}
of the asymptotic variance
\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}}
in
Theorem

3.2
, the
(1-\alpha)
confidence interval
\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
defined in (
11
) is (pointwise) asymptotically valid:

\lim_{n\to\infty}P(\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha})=1-\alpha.

Uniform validity

The pointwise validity in
Corollary

3.4
already carries some robustness to the unknown data-generating process: The proposed procedure is valid over the specification uncertainty class
\overline{\mathcal{C}}(P)
defined in
Section

4.1
. We show in
Section

B.3
that, under uniform analogues of
Assumptions

1
,
2
and
3
, this can be strengthened to uniform validity over a class
\mathcal{P}
, in the sense that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}|P(\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha})-(1-\alpha)|=0.

4
Additional technical results

4.1
Motivation for restricting to shared covariates and exact agreement

One rationale behind restricting the transfer weights
w
to depend only on the shared covariates is that it respects
Assumption

2
. The following lemma formalizes this, and also shows that solving the optimization problem (
3
) for arbitrary weights might not lead to a causal estimand.

Lemma 4.1
.

When
w=w(X_{S_{\cap}})
, any candidate adjustment set that is valid under
P
is also valid under
wP
. On the other hand, there exist examples where an arbitrary weighting
w=w(X)
yields no candidate adjustment set valid for the reweighted population
wP
, and solving the optimization problem (
3
) for arbitrary weights
w=w(X)
leads to a common estimand, as in (
4
), that has no causal interpretation.

Another key design choice in (
3
) is that we require the mean of the contrasts to exactly match on the reweighted population. To motivate this choice, we derive a lower bound on the minimax risk of estimating the ATE of the reweighted population
wP
under the specification uncertainty not resolved by the observed data law under
Assumptions

1
and
2
. To formalize this, define for each
1\leq k\leq K
the collection
4
4

4

A simple construction shows that each
\mathcal{C}_{k}(P)
is non-empty: Draw
(A,X)
from their marginal under
P
. If
A=a
, draw
Y(a)
from the observed conditional law of
Y
given
(A=a,X)
, and independently draw
Y(1-a)
from the observed conditional law of
Y
given
\smash{(A=1-a,X_{S_{k}})}
. With
Y=Y(A)
, the resulting
(Y,A,X)
has the same law as under
P
, and satisfies
(Y(0),Y(1))\perp\!\!\!\!\perp A
conditional on
\smash{X_{S_{k}}}
.

\mathcal{C}_{k}(P)
of all probability distributions of
(Y(0),Y(1),A,X)
that induce the same observed data law as
P
, and imply ignorability for the adjustment set
S_{k}
. Precisely,

\mathcal{C}_{k}(P):=\{Q:\mathcal{L}_{Q}(Y(A),A,X)=\mathcal{L}_{P}(Y(A),A,X)\text{ and }S_{k}\text{ is a valid adjustment under Q}\},

where
\mathcal{L}_{P}(U)
denotes the law of
U
under
P
.
The union
\overline{\mathcal{C}}(P):=\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{C}_{k}(P)
then collects all distributions of
(Y(0),Y(1),A,X)
that induce the same observed data-law as
P
and satisfy
Assumption

2
. The following result shows that the minimax risk over the specification-uncertainty class
\overline{\mathcal{C}}(P)
is bounded away from zero unless we exactly match the estimands.

Lemma 4.2
.

Fix any
w=w(X_{S_{\cap}})\geq 0
,
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w]=1
. Under
Assumption

2
, it holds that

\inf_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\,\sup_{Q\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}(P)}\left(a-\mathbb{E}_{wQ}[Y(1)-Y(0)]\right)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}\left(\max_{k}\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP)-\min_{k}\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP)\right)^{2},

where
\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP):=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau(X_{S_{k}})]
, with
\tau(X_{S})
as defined in (
1
).
Thus, the minimax risk is bounded away from zero unless
\max_{k}\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP)=\min_{k}\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP)
, in which case the minimax risk is zero and the ATE of the reweighted population is the unique minimizer.

In particular,
Lemma

4.2
implies that the ATE of a reweighted population defined in (
2
) is point identified if and only if the reweighting makes all candidate estimands the same. Among all such populations, we find the one closest (in KL divergence) to the original population.

4.2
Neyman orthogonality

Following the double/debiased machine learning literature
(
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
;
Chernozhukov et al., 2021
)
, the debiasing in
Theorem

3.2
can be motivated through Neyman orthogonality of the influence function. Let
\eta:=(\mu,e,m_{1},g,\lambda,\nu)
collect all the nuisance parameters, namely:

1.

the conditional response functions
\mu_{a}(X_{S_{k}})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\mid A=a,X_{S_{k}}]
,

2.

the propensity score functions
e(X_{S_{k}})=P(A=1\mid X_{S_{k}})
,

3.

the conditional contrasts
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
, reparametrized as
(m_{1},g)
where
g
is as in (
5
) and
m_{1}(X_{S_{\cap}}):=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
,

4.

the exponential tilt parameter
\lambda
that, together with
g
, defines the transfer weights as

w_{g,\lambda}(X_{S_{\cap}})\propto\exp(g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\lambda),

5.

the aggregation weights
\nu
summing to
1
, as used in (
9
).

Our proof of
Theorem

3.2
given in
Section

C.3
establishes the asymptotic linearity

\widehat{\tau}_{bc}^{\mathtt{SR}}-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}=\mathbb{P}_{n}[\psi(D;\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}},\eta)]+o_{p}(n^{-1/2}),

where
D=(Y,A,X)
, and the influence function
\psi(D;\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}},\eta)
is given by

\psi(D;\tau,\eta):=w_{g,\lambda}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\nu^{\top}\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu,e}(Y,A,X)-\tau\right.\\
+\left.\lambda^{\top}(\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu,e}(Y,A,X)-g(X_{S_{\cap}}))\left(m_{1}(X_{S_{\cap}})-g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\nu_{2:K}-\tau\right)\right),

(12)

where

\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu,e}(X):=\mu_{1}(X)-\mu_{0}(X)+\frac{A}{e(X)}(Y-\mu_{1}(X))-\frac{1-A}{1-e(X)}(Y-\mu_{0}(X))
,
and
\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X):=\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{1}(Y,A,X)-\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)
,
k=2,\dots,K
. Consequently, the asymptotic variance
\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}}
in
Theorem

3.2
is given by

\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}}={\rm Var}_{P}(\psi(D;\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}},\mu,e,m_{1},g,\lambda^{*},\nu^{*})),

(13)

with
\lambda^{*}
as in
Lemma

3.1
and the affine weights
\nu^{*}
defined as:
\nu^{*}_{1}=1-\sum_{k=2}^{K}\nu^{*}_{k}
and

\nu^{*}_{2:K}:=\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\right]\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(m_{1}(X_{S_{\cap}})-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right].

Next, we introduce the perturbation paths for the nuisance parameters and establish Neyman orthogonality in
Proposition

4.3
. Assume that for some
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[e^{(\|\lambda^{*}\|+\varepsilon)\|g(X_{S_{\cap}})\|}(1+|m_{1}(X_{S_{\cap}})|)\left\{1+|Y|+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{a=0}^{1}|\mu_{a}(X_{S_{k}})|\right\}\right]<\infty.

(14)

Fix measurable perturbation functions
h_{\mu,a,k}\left(X_{S_{k}}\right)
,
h_{e,k}\left(X_{S_{k}}\right)
,
h_{m_{1}}\left(X_{S_{\cap}}\right)
and
h_{g}\left(X_{S_{\cap}}\right)
, all bounded in absolute value (or norm) by a constant
H\in(0,\infty)
, and vectors
h_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}
and
h_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
satisfying
\sum_{k=1}^{K}h_{\nu,k}=0
. For
|t|<t_{0}:=\min\{1,\xi/(2H),\varepsilon/(2+2\|h_{\lambda}\|)\}
(with
\xi
as in
Assumption

2
), define

\displaystyle\mu_{a,t}\left(X_{S_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{a}\left(X_{S_{k}}\right)+t\cdot h_{\mu,a,k}\left(X_{S_{k}}\right),

\displaystyle e_{t}\left(X_{S_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle=e\left(X_{S_{k}}\right)+t\cdot h_{e,k}\left(X_{S_{k}}\right),

(15)

\displaystyle m_{1,t}\left(X_{S_{\cap}}\right)

\displaystyle=m_{1}\left(X_{S_{\cap}}\right)+t\cdot h_{m_{1}}\left(X_{S_{\cap}}\right),

\displaystyle g_{t}\left(X_{S_{\cap}}\right)

\displaystyle=g\left(X_{S_{\cap}}\right)+t\cdot h_{g}\left(X_{S_{\cap}}\right),

\displaystyle\lambda_{t}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{*}+t\cdot h_{\lambda},

\displaystyle\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=\nu^{*}+t\cdot h_{\nu}.

Proposition 4.3
.

Suppose that
Assumptions

1
,
2
and
3
hold true, and that the integrability condition (
14
) holds.
Then, with the nuisance paths as defined in (
15
), we have

\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\psi(D;\,\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}},\,\mu_{t},\,e_{t},\,m_{1,t},\,g_{t},\,\lambda_{t},\,\nu_{t})\right]\bigg|_{t=0}=0.

The above result complements
Theorem

3.2
by providing further insights into the debiasing. It
shows that the resulting influence function in (
12
) is robust to first-order perturbations of all the nuisance components. The proof of this result clarifies how the above choice of
\nu^{*}
and the bias-correction term jointly account for first-order errors from estimating
\lambda^{*}
and
g
.

4.3
Bounds on the ATE shift

While the proposed method provides sharp inference for the reweighted ATE, it is natural to ask how we can bound the gap
|\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}-\overline{\tau}|
between the ATE of the reweighted population and the original. This difference is
O(1)
in general, since we cannot test which adjustment sets satisfy ignorability. The following lemma gives an upper bound on this ATE shift.
Throughout this section, we denote the conditional contrasts by
{m_{k}}(X_{S_{\cap}}):=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
and their averages by
\overline{\tau}_{k}:=\mathbb{E}_{P}[{m_{k}}(X_{S_{\cap}})]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})]
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose that
Assumptions

2
,
1
and
3
hold, and that the conditional contrasts
{m_{k}}(X_{S_{\cap}})
are
\sigma^{2}
sub-Gaussian, i.e., for each
k=1,\dots,K
and every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, it holds that

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\exp\left(t\left({m_{k}}(X_{S_{\cap}})-\overline{\tau}_{k}\right)\right)\right]\leq\exp\left(\sigma^{2}t^{2}/2\right).

Then, the difference between the ATE of the new target population and the original satisfies

\left|\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}-\overline{\tau}\right|\leq\sigma\sqrt{2D_{\mathrm{KL}}(w^{*}P\,\|\,P)}.

The above bound is sharp, as shown in
Section

C.7
using an example with jointly Gaussian conditional contrasts.
Another natural question is how
|\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}-\overline{\tau}|
scales with the disagreements in the estimands
\overline{\tau}_{k}=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})]
. Define the vector of disagreements as

\Delta:=\mathbb{E}_{P}[g(X_{S_{\cap}})]=(\overline{\tau}_{1}-\overline{\tau}_{2},\dots,\overline{\tau}_{1}-\overline{\tau}_{K})^{\top}.

(16)

The following result provides a local upper bound on the difference
|\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}-\overline{\tau}|
in terms of the Mahalanobis distance of
\Delta
from zero.

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose that
Assumptions

2
,
1
and
3
hold along a sequence
\smash{\{P_{t}\}}
. Assume that the variances of the
conditional contrasts
\smash{m_{k}(X_{S_{\cap}})}
under
\smash{\{P_{t}\}}
are uniformly bounded and define
\Sigma_{g}:={\rm Var}(g(X_{S_{\cap}}))
. Assume also that the moment generating functions of
\smash{\Sigma_{g}^{-1/2}g(X_{S_{\cap}})}
under
\{P_{t}\}
are uniformly bounded in a radius
r>0
(independent of
t
). Then,

|\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}-\overline{\tau}|=O(\|\Sigma_{g}^{-1/2}\Delta\|),\quad\text{as}\quad\|\Sigma_{g}^{-1/2}\Delta\|\to 0.

In the special case where the conditional contrasts
\{{m_{k}}(X_{S_{\cap}})\}
are jointly Gaussian, we have the exact identity:
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}-\overline{\tau}=-\Delta^{\top}\Sigma_{g}^{-1}c_{j}
where
c_{j}:={\rm Cov}_{P}(g(X_{S_{\cap}}),{m_{j}}(X_{S_{\cap}}))
and
S_{j}
is a valid adjustment set (which exists under
Assumption

2
).

5
Practical considerations

5.1
Diagnostic plots

Our procedure shifts the target from the original population to a reweighted population. The resulting estimate should therefore be interpreted with respect to the new target population, which requires transparent reporting of how that population differs from the original.
To do this in practice, we recommend comparing the covariate distributions under the original and reweighted populations using histogram or density plots as in
Figures

3
and
2
and summary statistics (means, quantiles). With such diagnostic plots, one can assess whether the redefined population remains substantively meaningful.

There are two possible scenarios. When the reweighted population closely resembles the original, as in
Figure

2
for our real-data example, the interpretive cost of shifting the target is modest and the reweighted ATE might be of direct scientific interest. When the distribution shift is more pronounced, one should assess whether the reweighted population remains a meaningful target for the question at hand. In this case, our framework allows imposing additional constraints on the reweighting—requiring, for instance, that some pre-specified functions of the covariates retain the same mean as in the original population—so that the new target preserves features of the original population deemed important by the researcher. We describe this extension in the following section.

5.2
Protecting covariates

We present here an extension of the proposed method that enables the researcher to protect certain features of the original population. More precisely, consider any pre-specified function
f(X_{S_{\cap}})
of the covariates that define the reweighting; we aim to preserve its mean under the reweighting.
Formally, we solve the following problem:

w^{*}=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{w=w(X_{S_{\cap}})\geq 0,\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[w]=1}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(wP\,\|\,P)\quad\;\text{subject to}\\
\quad\mathbb{E}_{wP}[\tau(X_{S_{1}})]=\cdots=\mathbb{E}_{wP}[\tau(X_{S_{K}})],\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{wP}[f(X_{S_{\cap}})]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[f(X_{S_{\cap}})].

(17)

Our specification-robust approach developed in
Sections

2
and
3
extends naturally to the above problem, simply by appending
f-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f]
to the constraint vector
\Delta\tau(X)
defined in
Assumption

3
. That is, replace
\Delta\tau(X)
with the following:

\delta(X):=(\tau(X_{S_{1}})-\tau(X_{S_{2}}),\dots,\tau(X_{S_{1}})-\tau(X_{S_{K}}),f(X_{S_{\cap}})-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f(X_{S_{\cap}})]).

(18)

Similarly, we replace the function
g
defined in
Assumption

3
with the following:

g(X_{S_{\cap}}):=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\delta(X)\mid X_{S_{\cap}}\right].

(19)

We can now rewrite the optimization problem (
17
) more compactly as:

w^{*}=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{w=w(X_{S_{\cap}})\geq 0,\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[w]=1}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(wP\,\|\,P)\quad\;\text{subject to}\quad\mathbb{E}_{wP}[g(X_{S_{\cap}})]=0,

which bears enough similarity to our previous formulation (
3
) that
we can empirically solve the above problem in the same manner as in
Section

3.1
. The bias-correction, however, requires some modification to incorporate the additional constraints; we refer the reader to
Section

B.2.3

for a complete description. We denote by
\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}+}_{bc}
the resulting bias-corrected estimator, and the following result provides its asymptotic distribution.

Figure 4
:
An illustration of covariate protection in the 401(k) application (
Section

6.2
). Left panel: The marginal distributions of age without protection. Right panel: The reweighted marginal is visibly closer to the original when we additionally protect the mean and variance of age; see
Section

6.2
for a quantitative comparison.

Theorem 5.1

(Asymptotic normality under additional covariate constraints)
.

Assume that the conditions of
Theorem

3.2
hold when
g
is re-defined as in (
19
) and the differences-in-contrasts vector
\Delta\tau
is replaced with the constraint vector
\delta
in (
18
). Then,

\sqrt{n}\left(\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}+}_{bc}-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}+}),

where
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}
is the ATE of the new target population defined as the solution to (
17
). An explicit expression for the asymptotic variance
\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}+}
is deferred to
Section

B.2.3
.

Theorem

5.1
yields asymptotically valid confidence intervals for the ATE
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}
in the same manner as in
Corollary

3.4
of
Theorem

3.2
; we omit the analogous result here for space.

The protection extension developed above provides a way to act on the diagnostics from
Section

5.1
. For example, the diagnostic plot (
Figure

2
) in our real-data analysis shows that the marginal distribution of age shifts toward a younger population under the reweighting. For covariates exhibiting such distributional shifts, we recommend protecting the first few moments as a starting point. We illustrate this in
Figure

4
, where protecting the first two moments of age brings its reweighted marginal distribution visibly closer to the original; see
Section

6.2
for a quantitative comparison.

Protecting a richer collection of features naturally imposes more restrictions, which may eventually lead to infeasibility. We illustrate this in
Figure

5
where we successively protect all mixed moments up to order
k
. As
k
grows, the proposed confidence interval gradually widens and the KL divergence increases, and the problem becomes infeasible at
k=12
. This highlights a tradeoff between preserving additional features of the original population and retaining enough flexibility for the reweighting to reconcile the candidate estimands.

5.3
Infeasibility is informative

When some adjustment sets produce estimates that are drastically different from the rest, the optimization problem (
3
) might have no solution—that is, no reweighted population exists for which all candidate estimators agree. This infeasibility is, in fact, a meaningful finding: It highlights that the assumptions underlying the candidate adjustment sets might be fundamentally incompatible. In this scenario, our procedure can still be used to identify clusters of adjustment sets that are internally compatible, in the sense that for each cluster we can find a target population close to the original population such that all the adjustment sets
in that cluster
target the ATE of this new target population. This can serve as a starting point for the practitioner to deliberate on which group of assumptions is most credible for the application at hand.

Figure 5
:
Specification-robust inference under increasingly rich covariate protection in the 401(k) application. We successively protect all mixed moments of the common covariates up to order
k
. The left panel shows the 95% confidence intervals (numerical labels are widths, blue dots show point estimates), and the right panel reports the (out-of-fold) KL divergence from the original population. The optimization problem becomes infeasible at
k=12
.

6
Empirical illustrations

6.1
Simulation examples

Here we use simulation examples to empirically demonstrate the proposed method. These examples also illustrate the failure of naive remedies:
Example

1
shows that the intersection of candidate adjustment sets need not be valid, while
Example

2
shows that the union need not be valid either, and that even a pre-treatment covariate can be a non-confounder whose inclusion leads to invalid inference.

For each of these simulation examples, we consider two candidate adjustment sets:
S_{1}=\{X_{1}\}
and
S_{2}=\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
. We draw
n=1000
observations and compute 95% confidence intervals for the ATE obtained using the interacted linear regression models for both adjustment sets; see
Examples

1
and
2
below for the results from one replication. In particular, we note that in each of these examples the confidence intervals for the two adjustment sets lead to
conflicting inference
about the ATE.

Example 1

(Two confounders)
.

Draw
X_{1},X_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
A\mid X_{1},X_{2}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(1/(1+e^{5X_{1}+5X_{2}}))
,
Y=A\cdot(1+X_{1}-5X_{2})+4X_{2}+\varepsilon_{Y}
where
\varepsilon_{Y}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Here
\{X_{1}\}
is an invalid adjustment set while
\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
is valid. The true ATE is
\overline{\tau}=1
. The baseline method is the linear regression with treatment-covariate interactions.

For a single replication, the 95% bootstrap confidence intervals are
[-1.85,-1.14]
for the adjustment set
\{X_{1}\}
and
[0.81,\,1.21]
for the adjustment set
\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
. Applying our specification-robust procedure (the linear regression variant described in
Appendix

A
) results in a location shift of the distribution of
X_{1}
, namely,
\mathcal{N}(c,1)
where
c\approx 1.097
(see
Section

B.1
for details). The conditional distribution of
(X_{2},A,Y(0),Y(1))
given
X_{1}
remains unchanged under the reweighting, since
X_{2}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp X_{1}
and
w=w(X_{1})
. The reweighted population (
Figure

3
) has ATE
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\approx 2.097
and the proposed specification-robust CI is
[0.99,\,2.13]
.

Example 2

(M-bias)
.

Draw
U_{1},U_{2},X_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
A=\text{ $\mathbf{1}$}{\left\{U_{1}+X_{1}>0\right\}}
,
X_{2}=U_{1}+U_{2}
,
Y=A\cdot(1+0.5X_{1}-6U_{2})+5U_{2}+\varepsilon_{Y}
where
\varepsilon_{Y}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Here
\{X_{1}\}
is a valid adjustment set while
\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
is invalid (adjusting for
X_{2}
opens a backdoor path and introduces M-bias). The true ATE is
\overline{\tau}=1
. The baseline method is the linear regression with treatment-covariate interactions.

For a single replication, the 95% bootstrap confidence intervals are
[0.90,\,2.02]
for the adjustment set
\{X_{1}\}
and
[-1.30,-0.50]
for the adjustment set
\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
. Applying our specification-robust procedure (the linear regression variant described in
Appendix

A
) results in a location shift of the distribution of
X_{1}
, namely,
\mathcal{N}(c,1)
where
c=-\frac{2}{3}\sqrt{\pi}\approx-1.182
(see
Section

B.1
for details). The conditional distribution of
(X_{2},A,Y(0),Y(1))
given
X_{1}
remains unchanged under the reweighting, since
X_{2}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp X_{1}
and
w=w(X_{1})
. The reweighted population (
Figure

3
) has ATE
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\approx 0.409
and the proposed specification-robust CI is
[-0.38,\,0.83]
.

We run the above experiments for 1000 replications and report in
Table

1
the empirical coverage (proportion of times the CIs contain the true target) along with the average width (averaged across replications). Note that in both of these examples, the CIs constructed using an invalid adjustment set (which differs across the two examples) provide zero coverage, which highlights the consequences of a misspecified covariate adjustment.
Table

1
also verifies that both the convex hull and the proposed method provide nominal coverage for their respective target estimands. The proposed method leads to much tighter confidence intervals than the convex hull while changing the target to a shifted population as shown in
Figure

3
.

6.2
Application: Effect of 401(k) availability on financial assets

Here we empirically demonstrate the proposed procedure using the
pension
dataset from the R package
hdm

(
Chernozhukov et al., 2016
)
, also used as an application by
Chernozhukov et al. (2018)
. The sample consists of 9,915 households drawn from the fourth round of interviews in the 1990 panel of the Survey of Income and Program Participation
(
Chernozhukov and Hansen, 2004
)
. Our goal is to study the causal effect of 401(k) availability, defined as whether any household member works for an employer offering a 401(k) plan, on the net financial assets of that household.

The dataset includes a variety of covariates, including age, income, education, family size, marital status, home ownership, and whether the individual is covered by other pension or IRA plans. There is some ambiguity regarding which covariates should be included in the adjustment set. For instance, participation in the IRA is
plausibly
affected by 401(k) availability, making it potentially a post-treatment variable rather than a confounder.

A related ambiguity concerns income.
Following
Poterba et al. (1994)
;
Poterba et al. (1995)
,
Chernozhukov et al. (2018)
motivate adjusting for income by arguing that workers primarily based their job choices on income and other job characteristics rather than 401(k) availability. However,
Ippolito (1997)
emphasizes that workers with stronger preferences for saving may select into jobs that offer 401(k) plans.
If 401(k) availability influences employment choices and the associated earnings, contemporaneous income could be downstream of that availability. In this dataset, income and 401(k) availability are recorded from the same round of interviews, leaving their causal ordering uncertain. Given the usual caution against adjusting for post-treatment variables, a researcher concerned about this possibility may exclude income, while another may regard it as an essential confounder.

To demonstrate our method, we consider an example with four adjustment sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{4}
constructed in a nested manner:
S_{1}=
{age, education},
S_{2}=S_{1}\,\cup
{family size, income},
S_{3}=S_{2}\,\cup
{marital status, two-earner household}, and
S_{4}=S_{3}\,\cup
{home-ownership, defined pension, participation in IRA}.
As the baselines, we construct AIPW confidence intervals based on each of the candidate adjustment sets. Our implementation
of
Algorithm

1
uses generalized random forests (
grf
package;
Wager and Athey, 2018
;
Athey et al., 2019
) and 2-fold cross-fitting to learn the nuisance functions.

Figure 6
:
95% CIs for the ATE of 401(k) availability on net financial assets (in thousands of US dollars). The black lines show AIPW intervals for four nested adjustment sets, the red line shows the convex hull of the AIPW CIs, and the green line shows the proposed specification-robust CI. The circles indicate corresponding point estimates.

Figure

6
shows the 95% confidence intervals obtained using each of these adjustment sets (in black), the naive confidence interval formed by the convex hull of the different AIPW confidence intervals (in red) and the confidence interval constructed using our specification-robust approach (in green). The proposed specification-robust confidence interval in this example is about
77% narrower
compared to the convex hull. This demonstrates how our specification-robust procedure can be a useful alternative to committing to one adjustment set without guaranteed coverage or simply reporting the whole range.

Figure

2
provides a visualization of the reweighted population in this example using the marginal distributions of the covariates age, income, and education. We note that the marginal histograms of education and income for the reweighted population are visibly close to those of the original population, while the reweighted age distribution shows a shift toward younger individuals.
We further illustrate in
Figure

4
that protecting the mean and variance of age brings its marginal histogram visibly closer to that of the original population than in the unprotected case. Quantitatively, this reduces the KL divergence of the marginal distribution of age (in the reweighted population vs the original) by 37% compared to the unprotected case, while paying a modest 6.6% increase in the total divergence.

7
Discussion

In observational studies, researchers often face multiple plausible adjustment sets, none of which can be confirmed as valid using the data alone. This poses a practical challenge, since different adjustment sets may yield conflicting conclusions and reporting the whole range of estimates does not resolve the ambiguity. Simple remedies such as adjusting for the union or intersection of the candidate adjustment sets, or restricting to pre-treatment covariates, can also fail, as we illustrate in
Examples

2
and
1
.

We introduce a specification-robust procedure that returns a single point estimate and one confidence interval for the average treatment effect, which are valid when at least one candidate adjustment set satisfies ignorability—without requiring knowledge of which one.
Similar to how overlap weighting or trimming handle overlap violations, we resolve specification uncertainty by changing the target to a population where credible, precise inference is feasible.
We focus on a reweighted population where all candidate estimands agree, chosen as close as possible to the original population in KL-divergence.
Section

5.1
recommends diagnostics for assessing this population shift, and
Section

5.2
provides an extension that can preserve pre-specified features of the original population.

The proposed method has a few limitations worth noting. First, it requires that at least one adjustment set satisfies ignorability; it does not protect against the case where all candidate sets are invalid. Second, there could be settings where the candidate adjustment sets have no covariate in common, in which case our proposed method is not applicable.
Third, the candidate adjustment sets may be fundamentally incompatible—in the sense that no reweighted population exists for which all estimands agree. We discuss in
Section

5.3
how to handle this infeasibility issue by considering smaller clusters of the candidate adjustment sets.
Another limitation is the interpretability of the transfer weights for individual units. In practice, binary weights can be easier to interpret and can yield a more transparent description of the resulting target population. However, extending our framework to binary weights is both analytically and computationally challenging, and beyond our current scope.

Another scope limitation concerns heavy-tailed contrasts, which may result in extreme transfer weights. This can be diagnosed by plotting the fitted contrasts and the fitted transfer weights. In such cases, trimming or winsorizing are practical remedies to stabilize the procedure. More fundamentally, the exponential-moment restriction arises from the exponential tilting induced by the KL divergence, and extending our procedure to other divergences may permit weaker tail conditions and thus accommodate heavy-tailed contrasts.

It would also be worthwhile to extend the proposed procedure to settings with many candidate adjustment sets, where requiring exact agreement of the estimands may be overly restrictive. In such scenarios, it might be beneficial to relax our constraints by allowing approximate alignment of the estimands, or requiring only a qualitative agreement (e.g., same sign of the causal effect). We leave these directions for future research.

8

The authors thank Guido Imbens, Sam Pimentel, Peng Ding, and Stefan Wager for insightful discussions, and the participants of the Berkeley-Stanford Joint Colloquium and Stanford Statistics Department retreat for their valuable feedback. Rothenhäusler acknowledges financial support from the Dieter Schwarz Foundation, the Dudley Chamber Fund, and the David Huntington Foundation.

Appendices

Appendix A
Specification-robust inference with parametric baseline

The linear regression model with treatment-covariate interactions
(
Lin, 2013
)
is one of the most commonly used parametric approaches for drawing inference on the ATE in applied research, often preferred for its straightforward implementation or transparent interpretability of the coefficients; see discussions in
Hainmueller et al. (2019, Section 1)
and
Anoke et al. (2019, Section 3.1)
.

In this section, we consider the problem of drawing inference with multiple adjustment sets using the linear model with treatment-covariate interactions as the baseline method for estimating the contrasts
\tau(X_{S_{k}})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\mid A=1,X_{S_{k}}]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\mid A=0,X_{S_{k}}]
. Throughout this section, we work under the assumption that such an interacted linear model is well-specified for at least one of the adjustment sets.

Assumption A.1
.

Assume that
j\in\{1,2,\dots,K\}
is such that the adjustment set
S_{j}
is valid and the data
(Y(0),Y(1),A,X)
generated from
P
satisfy the following:

\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y(a)\mid X_{S_{j}}]=\overline{\delta}+X_{S_{j}}^{\top}\overline{\beta}+a\cdot(\overline{\tau}+X_{S_{j}}^{\top}\overline{\gamma}),\quad a=0,1.

Assume further that (i) the covariates are centered, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}_{P}[X]=0
, (ii) the adjustment sets have non-empty intersection:
S_{\cap}=\cap_{k=1}^{K}S_{k}\neq\emptyset
, (iii) the design matrix for each regression is nonsingular, and (iv)
\mathbb{E}_{P}[|Y|^{4}+\|X\|^{4}]<\infty
.

Empirically, we obtain
\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})
by fitting a linear regression with interaction between the treatment
A
and the covariates
X_{S_{k}}
. Thus,

\begin{split}\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})&:=\widehat{\tau}_{k}+X_{S_{k}}^{\top}\widehat{\gamma}_{k},\\
(\widehat{\delta}_{k},\,\widehat{\beta}_{k},\,\widehat{\tau}_{k},\,\widehat{\gamma}_{k})&:=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\delta,\,\beta,\,\tau,\,\gamma}\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(Y_{i}-\delta-X_{i,S_{k}}^{\top}\beta-A_{i}(\tau+X_{i,S_{k}}^{\top}\gamma)\right)^{2}.\end{split}

(A.1)

Even when the adjustment set is possibly invalid, it is well-known (see, e.g.,
Buja et al. (2019, Proposition 7.1)
) that
\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})
targets the following estimand:

\begin{split}\tau^{*}(X_{S_{k}})&:=\tau_{k}^{*}+X_{S_{k}}^{\top}\gamma_{k}^{*},\\
(\delta_{k}^{*},\,\beta_{k}^{*},\,\tau_{k}^{*},\,\gamma_{k}^{*})&:=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\delta,\,\beta,\,\tau,\,\gamma}\ \mathbb{E}_{P}\left(Y-\delta-X_{S_{k}}^{\top}\beta-A(\tau+X_{S_{k}}^{\top}\gamma)\right)^{2}.\end{split}

(A.2)

Under
Assumption

A.1
,
\tau_{j}^{*}
is the same as the ATE
\overline{\tau}
(and
\gamma_{j}^{*}=\overline{\gamma}
). However,
\tau_{k}^{*}
does not have a causal interpretation when the adjustment set
S_{k}
is invalid. Our core approach remains the same as in
Section

3
, except that here we aim to solve the population optimization problem (
3
) with
\tau^{*}(X_{S_{k}})
instead of
\tau(X_{S_{k}})
. Thus, we find a reweighted population
w^{*}P
closest in KL-divergence to the original population
P
such that

\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau^{*}(X_{S_{1}})]=\cdots=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau^{*}(X_{S_{K}})].

The following lemma mirrors
Lemma

2.2
for the setting of this section and shows that estimand in the above display has a causal interpretation—it is the ATE of the reweighted population; see

Section

C.10

for a proof.

Lemma A.1

(Interpretation of the new estimand)
.

Suppose that the adjustment sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{K}
satisfy
Assumption

A.1
and
w^{*}=w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})
solves the optimization problem

w^{*}=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{w=w(X_{S_{\cap}})\geq 0,\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[w]=1}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(wP\,\|\,P)\quad\text{subject to}\quad\mathbb{E}_{wP}[\tau^{*}(X_{S_{1}})]=\cdots=\mathbb{E}_{wP}[\tau^{*}(X_{S_{K}})].

(A.3)

Then, for each
k=1,2,\dots,K
, it holds that

\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau^{*}(X_{S_{k}})]=\mathbb{E}_{w^{*}P}[Y(1)-Y(0)]=\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}.

A.1
Existence and uniqueness of the transfer weights

Here we derive a result analogous to
Lemma

3.1
for the setting of this section by replacing
\tau(X_{S_{k}})
with
\tau^{*}(X_{S_{k}})
in
Lemma

3.1
. We state this result as
Lemma

A.2
below, which shows the existence and uniqueness of the weighting function
w^{*}
in (
A.3
) under the following heterogeneity assumption.

Assumption A.2
.

Define differences-in-contrasts
\Delta\tau_{k}^{*}(X):=\tau^{*}(X_{S_{1}})-\tau^{*}(X_{S_{k}})
for
k=2,\dots,K
, where
\tau^{*}(X_{S_{k}})
are as defined in (
A.2
), and denote
\Delta\tau^{*}:=(\Delta\tau_{2}^{*},\dots,\Delta\tau_{K}^{*})^{\top}
. Define regression on the shared covariates as

g^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}}):=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\Delta\tau^{*}(X)\mid X_{S_{\cap}}],\quad\psi_{P}(\lambda):=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\exp(\lambda^{\top}g^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}}))]\in(0,\infty].

Assume that (i) there is no
v\neq 0
such that
v^{\top}g^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\leq 0
almost surely under
P
, and (ii) the infimum of
\lambda\mapsto\psi_{P}(\lambda)
is not attained on the boundary of
\{\lambda:\psi_{P}(\lambda)<\infty\}
.

Lemma A.2

(Existence and uniqueness of the transfer weights)
.

Under
Assumption

A.2
, there exists a unique, finite
\lambda^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}
such that

\lambda^{*}=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}}\ \psi_{P}(\lambda).

(A.4)

Furthermore, the problem (
A.3
) has a unique (
P
-a.s.) solution given by the exponential tilt:

w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})={\exp\big(g^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\lambda^{*}\big)}\;\big/\;\psi_{P}(\lambda^{*}).

A.2
Estimation and inference

In light of
Lemma

A.2
, solving the problem (
A.3
) reduces to solving the convex problem (
A.4
), for which we need to estimate the conditional means
g_{k}^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau^{*}(X_{S_{1}})-\tau^{*}(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
. It turns out that
Assumptions

A.2
and
A.1
are not enough to enable us to estimate
g(X_{S_{\cap}})
at parametric rate, which is why we further impose the following modeling assumption.

Assumption A.3
.

For each
k=1,\dots,K
, there exist constants
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
such that

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau^{*}(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]=a_{k}+X_{S_{\cap}}^{\top}b_{k}.

We describe in
Algorithm

A.1
below a simplified version of our general procedure (
Algorithm

1
) proposed in
Section

3.2
that operates under
Assumptions

1
,
A.2
,
A.1
and
A.3
. We retain the name
specification-robust
for this model-based version of our estimator, as it remains robust without us knowing which regression adjustment set
S_{j}
satisfies
Assumption

A.1
.

The following theorem states the asymptotic distribution of the specification-robust linear model-based estimator
\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR,lm}}
we compute in
Algorithm

A.1
; see

Section

C.12

for a proof. It is important to note that this variant of our specification-robust procedure does not require any bias-correction. However, it relies on the stronger assumption that at least one of the linear models is correctly specified (
Assumption

A.1
) and that the conditional contrasts satisfy
Assumption

A.3
.

Theorem A.3

(Asymptotic normality of the specification-robust estimator, parametric variant)
.

Suppose that
Assumptions

1
,
A.2
,
A.1
and
A.3
hold, and assume that
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\exp(r\|X_{S_{\cap}}\|)]<\infty
for some
r>2\|\sum_{k=2}^{K}\lambda_{k}^{*}(b_{1}-b_{k})\|
(i.e., twice the slope of the affine map
X_{S_{\cap}}\mapsto g^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\lambda^{*}
). Then,

\sqrt{n}\left(\widehat{\tau}^{\,\mathtt{SR,lm}}-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR,lm}}),

for some
\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR,lm}}<\infty
. An explicit formula of
\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR,lm}}
is deferred to (
C.37
).

Algorithm A.1

Parametric variant of the proposed specification-robust procedure

1:

Data
(Y_{i},A_{i},X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
;
K\geq 2
adjustment sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{K}
with intersection
S_{\cap}\neq\emptyset
.

2:

An estimator
\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR,lm}}
of the ATE of the reweighted population defined in
Lemma

A.1
.

3:

Estimate
\tau^{*}(X_{S_{k}})
using the linear regression of
Y
on treatment
A
, covariates
X_{S_{k}}
and the treatment-covariate interactions.

4:

Run linear regression of
\widehat{\tau}^{*}(X_{S_{k}})
on
X_{S_{\cap}}
and obtain
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P}[\widehat{\tau}^{*}(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]=\widehat{a}_{k}+X_{S_{\cap}}^{\top}\widehat{b}_{k}
.

5:

Compute
\widehat{g}_{k}(X_{S_{\cap}})=\widehat{a}_{1}-\widehat{a}_{k}+X_{S_{\cap}}^{\top}(\widehat{b}_{1}-\widehat{b}_{k})
,
k=2,\dots,K
, and solve the convex problem:

\widehat{\lambda}:=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}}\,\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\exp\left(\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\lambda\right)\right].

6:

Compute the transfer weight for the
i
-th unit as

\widehat{w}(X_{i,S_{\cap}})={\exp\left(\widehat{g}(X_{i,S_{\cap}})^{\top}\widehat{\lambda}\right)}\,\Big/\,{\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\exp\left(\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\widehat{\lambda}\right)\right]}.

7:

Compute the specification-robust estimator:

\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR,lm}}=\widehat{a}_{1}+\widehat{b}_{1}^{\,\top}\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})X_{S_{\cap}}\right].

Corollary A.4

(specification-robust confidence intervals based on linear regression)
.

Given any consistent estimator
\widehat{\mathrm{V}}_{\mathtt{SR,lm}}
of the asymptotic variance

\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR,lm}}
in
Theorem

A.3
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}P\left(\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\in\left[\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR,lm}}-\frac{z_{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{V}}_{\mathtt{SR,lm}}},\ \widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR,lm}}+\frac{z_{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{V}}_{\mathtt{SR,lm}}}\,\right]\right)=1-\alpha,

where
z_{\alpha}
is the
(1-\alpha)
-th quantile of the standard normal distribution.

Remark A.5

(Variance estimation)
.

We recommend estimating
\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR,lm}}
either using the plug-in estimates in the explicit formula (
C.37
) (replacing
\theta^{*}
by
\widehat{\theta}
and expectations by
empirical averages),
or using the following bootstrap procedure.

1.

Choose a large number
B=B(n)
and for
b=1,\dots,B
, sample
D_{1}^{b},\dots,D_{n}^{b}
with replacement from the data
\{D_{1},\dots,D_{n}\}
where
D_{i}=(Y_{i},\,X_{i},\,A_{i})
.

2.

Compute the specification-robust linear model-based estimator
\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR,lm}}_{b}
using the resampled data and compute

\widehat{\mathrm{V}}_{\mathtt{SR,lm}}=\frac{n}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\left(\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR,lm}}_{b}-\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR,lm}}\right)^{2}.

Since our proof of
Theorem

A.3
is based on asymptotic linear expansions, this bootstrap procedure can be theoretically justified under suitable conditions. We demonstrate the empirical performance of the bootstrap confidence intervals constructed using
Remark

A.5
for our simulation examples in
Section

6.1
.

Remark A.6

(Affine weights are redundant)
.

It follows from the empirical estimating equations that the estimators
(\widehat{a}_{k}+\widehat{b}_{k}^{\,\top}\mathbb{P}_{n}[\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})X_{S_{\cap}}])
computed in
Algorithm

A.1
are numerically the same for each
k
. This is why the final step in
Algorithm

A.1
computes the specification-robust estimate only using the reference adjustment set, instead of an affine aggregation as in
Algorithm

1
.

Appendix B
Deferred results and details

B.1
Distribution shift in the simulation examples

In this section, we provide additional numerical details on the simulation examples discussed in
Section

6.1
, including the explicit transfer weights and the reweighted ATE. In both examples, we have:

•

K=2
candidate adjustments, namely
\{X_{1}\}
and
\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
.

•

X_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, and
X_{2}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp X_{1}
and
X_{2}
is also Gaussian with mean zero.

•

The conditional distribution of
(X_{2},A,Y(0),Y(1))
given
X_{1}
remains unchanged under the reweighting, since
w=w(X_{1})
and
X_{2}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp X_{1}
.

•

Both
Assumptions

A.2
and
A.3
hold true. Write
