g^{*}(X_{1})=\alpha+\beta X_{1}
where
\alpha=a_{1}-a_{2}
and
\beta=b_{1}-b_{2}\neq 0
, with
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}
as defined in
Assumption

A.3
.

The following result shows that the reweighting in both
Examples

1
and
2
results in a location shift for
X_{1}
; see
Section

C.13
for a proof.

Lemma B.1
.

Suppose that
K=2
and
g^{*}(X_{1})=\alpha+\beta X_{1}
with
\beta\neq 0
. Then, for
X_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,

\lambda^{*}=-\frac{\alpha}{\beta^{2}},\quad w^{*}(X_{1})=\exp\left(cX_{1}-\frac{c^{2}}{2}\right),\quad c:=\lambda^{*}\beta=-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}.

Thus,
X_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(c,1)
under
w^{*}P
. Moreover, the reweighted ATE is given by
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}=a_{1}+b_{1}c=a_{2}+b_{2}c
where
a_{k},b_{k}
are as defined in
Assumption

A.3
.

In light of
Lemma

B.1
, what remains is the computation of the regression coefficients
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}
. Doing so leads to explicit formula for the transfer weights and reweighted ATE in
Examples

1
and
2
which we provide in the following results, respectively; see
Sections

C.14
and
C.15
for proofs.

Lemma B.2
.

In
Example

1
, it holds that

g^{*}(X_{1})=\frac{3p}{1-p^{2}}+\frac{5p^{2}}{1-p^{2}}X_{1}
,
where
p=\mathbb{E}_{P}[X_{1}\mid A=1]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[X_{2}\mid A=1]
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\lambda^{*}=-\frac{3(1-p^{2})}{25p^{3}},\quad w^{*}(X_{1})=\exp\left(-\frac{3}{5p}X_{1}-\frac{9}{50p^{2}}\right),\quad c=-\frac{3}{5p},\qquad\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}=1-\frac{3}{5p}.

The constant
p
in
Lemma

B.2
, equivalently expressed as
p=\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[2X_{1}/(1+e^{5X_{1}+5X_{2}})\big]
, has no closed form. Numerical approximations give
p\approx-0.547
, and thus
c\approx 1.097
and
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\approx 2.097
.

Lemma B.3
.

In
Example

2
, we have

g^{*}(X_{1})=(4\sqrt{\pi}+6X_{1})/(2\pi-3)
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\lambda^{*}=-\frac{\sqrt{\pi}(2\pi-3)}{9},\quad w^{*}(X_{1})=\exp\left(-\frac{2\sqrt{\pi}}{3}X_{1}-\frac{2\pi}{9}\right),\quad c=-\frac{2\sqrt{\pi}}{3},\qquad\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}=1-\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{3}.

B.2
Further details on implementation

B.2.1
Parametric baseline

For the simulation examples discussed in
Section

6.1
, we estimate the conditional response functions using linear regression with treatment-covariate interactions, and construct specification-robust confidence intervals via the nonparametric bootstrap, as outlined in
Corollary

A.4
and
Remark

A.5
.

As outlined in
Appendix

A
, this procedure does not require additional bias-correction for the transfer weights, but it does rely on the stronger assumption that at least one of the candidate linear models is correctly specified. It is also worthwhile to note that
Algorithm

A.1
and
Theorem

A.3
do not require cross-fitting.

B.2.2
Nonparametric baseline

For the 401(k) application discussed in
Section

6.2
, we estimate the conditional response functions and propensity scores using generalized random forests (
grf
package;
Wager and Athey, 2018
;
Athey et al., 2019
), and use the plug-in variance estimator of
Remark

B.4
below to construct specification-robust confidence intervals.

Remark B.4

(Plug-in estimator of variance)
.

In practice, we recommend using a plug-in estimator of the asymptotic variance
\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}}
in
Theorem

3.2
, as follows.

\widehat{\mathrm{V}}_{\mathtt{SR}}:=\widehat{{\rm Var}}\left[\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k}\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}\right)\right.\\
+\left.\widehat{\lambda}^{\top}\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})(\Delta\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}(Y,A,X)-\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}}))\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\left[\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}\right]-\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}\right)\right],

where
\widehat{{\rm Var}}
denotes empirical variance.

This complements our construction of an asymptotically valid confidence interval in
Corollary

3.4
, and the resulting confidence interval demonstrates robust empirical performance, providing nominal coverage in our simulation examples in
Section

6
.

B.2.3
Protection extension

In
Section

5.2
, we discuss an extension of our specification-robust procedure which enables the researcher to protect (known) functions of the common covariates, with the population-level optimization problem as described in (
17
).
In this section, we provide the relevant algorithm (a modification of
Algorithm

1
) that solves the optimization problem (
17
) empirically, and computes the bias-corrected specification-robust estimator
\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}+}_{bc}
incorporating the additional constraints.
Theorem

5.1
states the asymptotic normality of this estimator but defers an explicit expression for the asymptotic variance, which we provide in the following remark; see
Section

C.9
for a proof.

Algorithm B.1

Specification-robust estimator for the ATE with additional covariate constraints (using externally fit nonparametric regressors)

1:

Data
(Y_{i},A_{i},X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
;
K\geq 2
adjustment sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{K}
with non-empty intersection
S_{\cap}
;
an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued function
f
of the covariates that we want to protect as in (
17
); propensity score estimators
\widehat{e}(X_{S_{k}})
of
e(X_{S_{k}})=P(A=1\mid X_{S_{k}})
, conditional response function estimators
\widehat{\mu}_{a}(X_{S_{k}})
of
\mu_{a}(X_{S_{k}})={\mathbb{E}}_{P}[Y\mid A=a,X_{S_{k}}]
, and regressors
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
estimating
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
for
a=0,1
, and
k=1,\dots,K
.

2:

An estimator
\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}+}_{bc}
of the ATE
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}
of the reweighted population
w^{*}P
defined via (
17
).

3:

Set
\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})=\widehat{\mu}_{1}(X_{S_{k}})-\widehat{\mu}_{0}(X_{S_{k}})
and estimate the constraint vector

\delta(X)
defined in (
18
) using

\widehat{\delta}(X):=\left(\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{1}})-\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{2}}),\,\dots,\,\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{1}})-\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{K}}),\,f(X_{S_{\cap}})-\mathbb{P}_{n}[f(X_{S_{\cap}})]\right).

4:

Use nonparametric regressors to compute
\widehat{g}_{k}(X_{S_{\cap}}):=\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[\widehat{\delta}_{k}(X)\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
, for
k=1,\dots,K-1
; set
\widehat{g}_{K:(K-1+d)}(X_{S_{\cap}}):=f(X_{S_{\cap}})-\mathbb{P}_{n}[f(X_{S_{\cap}})]
.

5:

Find transfer weights using the above choice of
\widehat{g}
, obtain
\widehat{\lambda}
as a by-product, and compute reweighted AIPW estimators
\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}
as in
Algorithm

1
.

6:

Find weights
\widehat{\nu}
as in
Algorithm

1
using the above
\widehat{g}
, i.e., set
\widehat{\nu}_{1}:=1-\sum_{k=2}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k}
and

\widehat{\nu}_{2:(K+d)}:=\left(\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\right]\right)^{-1}\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P}[\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]-\widehat{\tau}_{1}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right].

7:

With
\widehat{\delta}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}:=\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{1}(Y,A,X)-\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k+1}(Y,A,X)
for
1\leq k\leq K-1
, and
\widehat{\delta}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}:=\widehat{g}_{k}(X_{S_{\cap}})
for
K\leq k\leq K-1+d
, compute a bias-correction term as

\begin{split}B_{n}:=\widehat{\lambda}^{\top}\,\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\widehat{\delta}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}(Y,A,X)-\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\left(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}\right]-\widehat{\tau}_{1}^{\mathtt{R}}-\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\widehat{\nu}_{2:(K+d)}\right)\right]\\
\,-\,\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(f(X_{S_{\cap}})-\mathbb{P}_{n}[f(X_{S_{\cap}})]\right)^{\top}\widehat{\nu}_{(K+1):(K+d)}\right].\end{split}

8:

With
\widehat{\nu}
and
B_{n}
as defined above, compute
\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}+}_{bc}:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k}\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\mathtt{\,R}}+B_{n}
.

Remark B.5
.

The asymptotic variance
\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}+}
in
Theorem

5.1
is given by

\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}+}:={\rm Var}\Bigg[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\Bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu^{*}_{k}\,\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\lambda^{*,\top}(\delta^{\mathtt{AIPW}}(Y,A,X)-g(X_{S_{\cap}}))\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\tau(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}\right]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}-g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\nu_{2:(K+d)}^{*}\right)\Bigg)\\
-\left(w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})-1\right)(f(X_{S_{\cap}})-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f(X_{S_{\cap}})])^{\top}\nu_{(K+1):(K+d)}^{*}\Bigg],

where the vector
\delta^{\mathtt{AIPW}}(Y,A,X)
is the same as the constraint vector
\delta(X)
defined in (
18
) except with contrasts
\tau(X_{S_{k}})
replaced with their AIPW versions
\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)
(cf.
Theorem

3.2
), and the weights
\nu^{*}
are such that
\nu^{*}_{1}=1-\sum_{k=2}^{K}\nu^{*}_{k}
and

\nu^{*}_{2:(K+d)}:=\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\right]\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\tau(X_{S_{1}})-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}\right)\right].

B.3
Uniform validity

In this section, we establish that the specification-robust confidence intervals from
Section

3.2
are uniformly valid over a suitable class of distributions, provided the regularity conditions hold uniformly.
First, we set a general notation for the model class where we do not know which adjustment set is valid:

\mathcal{P}_{\mathtt{SR}}:=\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{P}_{k},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{P}_{k}:=\{P:{S_{k}}\text{ is a valid adjustment set under }P\}.

In other words,
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is the set of all distributions
P
of
(Y_{i}(0),Y_{i}(1),A_{i},X_{i})
(cf.
Assumption

1
) such that the ignorability condition
A\perp\!\!\!\!\perp Y(a)\mid X_{S_{k}}

(a=0,1)
holds under
P
. The substantive difference from the classes introduced in
Section

4.1
is that here the observed data law is allowed to change. We view the average treatment effect (ATE) as a functional:

\overline{\tau}(P):=\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y(1)-Y(0)].

(B.1)

On
\mathcal{P}_{\mathtt{SR}}
, the original ATE
\overline{\tau}(P)
is not point-identified unless the researcher knows which of the
K
adjustment sets is (are) valid. We shift the goalpost and set the following as the target estimand:

\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}(P):=\overline{\tau}(w^{*}P),

where the reweighted population
w^{*}P
is the closest—in KL divergence—to the original population where all candidate adjustment sets agree (see
Section

3
for details). Thus, even the ‘pointwise’ results derived in
Section

3
have a sense of uniform validity: While the standard ATE estimators are only valid on a single
\mathcal{P}_{k}
, our approach is valid on the union class
\mathcal{P}_{\mathtt{SR}}
(modulo our regularity conditions). In the rest of this section, we derive
results that are uniformly valid over a restricted class
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\mathtt{SR}}
, under suitable conditions, as follows.

Assumption B.1

(Uniform version of
Assumption

1
)
.

(Y_{i}(0),Y_{i}(1),A_{i},X_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,n
, are independent observations from an unknown distribution
P
that belongs to the class
\mathcal{P}
.

Assumption B.2

(Uniform version of
Assumption

2
)
.

Assume that
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\mathtt{SR}}
so that under every
P\in\mathcal{P}
, at least one of the candidate adjustment sets is valid. Assume further that the intersection
S_{\cap}
is non-empty and that we have strong overlap: For every
P\in\mathcal{P}
, and every
1\leq k\leq K
, it holds that
\xi\leq P(A=1\mid X_{S_{k}})\leq 1-\xi
almost surely, for some
\xi\in(0,1)
independent of
P
.

Similar to (
B.1
), denote contrasts as
\tau(X_{S_{k}};P):=\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\mid X_{S_{k}},A=1]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\mid X_{S_{k}},A=0]
, and differences-in-contrasts as
\Delta\tau_{k}(X;P):=\tau(X_{S_{1}};P)-\tau(X_{S_{k}};P)
for
k=2,\dots,K
, and write
\Delta\tau:=(\Delta\tau_{2},\dots,\Delta\tau_{K})^{\top}
. Also define

g(X_{S_{\cap}};P):=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\Delta\tau(X;P)\mid X_{S_{\cap}}],\quad\psi_{P}(\lambda):=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\exp(\lambda^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}};P))]\in(0,\infty].

(B.2)

Furthermore, denote by
Q_{\lambda,P}
the exponentially tilted distribution with density proportional to
\exp(\lambda^{\top}g)
w.r.t.
P
, i.e.,

\frac{dQ_{\lambda,P}}{dP}:=\exp(\lambda^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}};P))\;\Big/\;\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\exp(\lambda^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}};P))\right],

(B.3)

and recall from
Lemma

3.1
that the solution to the problem (
3
), unique
P
-a.s., is given by

w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}};P)=dQ_{\lambda^{*}(P),\,P}/dP
where
\lambda^{*}(P)
is defined in (
B.4
) below.

Assumption B.3

(Uniform version of
Assumption

3
)
.

There exist constants
\zeta>0
and
c_{0}\in(0,1]
such that, for every
P\in\mathcal{P}
and every unit vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}
,

P\big(v^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}};P)\geq\zeta\big)\geq c_{0}.

The following lemma mirrors
Lemma

3.1
. It states the existence and uniqueness of a finite minimizer
\lambda^{*}(P)
of the moment generating function
\psi_{P}
defined in (
B.2
), and also shows that
\|\lambda^{*}(P)\|
remains uniformly bounded; see
Section

C.16
for a proof.

Lemma B.6

(Uniform extension of
Lemma

3.1
)
.

Under
Assumption

B.3
, for every
P\in\mathcal{P}
the moment generating function
\psi_{P}
defined in (
B.2
) has a unique, finite minimizer
\lambda^{*}(P)
, i.e.,

\lambda^{*}(P)=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\lambda\,\in\,\mathbb{R}^{K-1}}\psi_{P}(\lambda).

(B.4)

Moreover,
\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\|\lambda^{*}(P)\|\leq\log(1/c_{0})/\zeta<\infty
where
c_{0}
and
\zeta
are constants as in
Assumption

B.3
.

It is worth noting that
Assumption

B.3
does not include an analogue of
Assumption

3
(ii) (that
\lambda^{*}(P)
lies in the interior of the effective domain
\{\lambda:\psi_{P}(\lambda)<\infty\}
), since
Lemma

3.1
already makes the connection clear, and the main purpose of this section is to derive uniform validity—which is the content of
Theorem

B.7
below; see
Section

C.17
for a proof. Indeed, condition (4) of
Theorem

B.7
states that
\psi_{P}
is finite in a radius
r>\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\|\lambda^{*}(P)\|
around the origin, which implies that
\lambda^{*}(P)
lies in the interior of
\{\lambda:\psi_{P}(\lambda)<\infty\}
for every
P\in\mathcal{P}
and thus the reweighting
w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}};P)
as defined above is the unique (
P
-a.s.) solution to (
3
) for every
P\in\mathcal{P}
, due to
Lemma

3.1
.

Theorem B.7

(Uniform version of
Theorem

3.2
)
.

Assume that the propensity score estimators
\widehat{e}(X_{S_{k}})
, the conditional response function estimators
\widehat{\mu}_{a}(X_{S_{k}})
and the iterated regression estimators
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P}[\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
used in
Algorithm

1
are fit independent of the data
(Y_{i},A_{i},X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, and that
Assumptions

B.1
,
B.2
and
B.3
hold true. Assume further that,

(1)

(uniform rates of the nonparametric regressors) for
a=0,1
and
k=1,\dots,K
,

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\|\widehat{\mu}_{a}(X_{S_{k}})-\mu_{a}(X_{S_{k}})\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}=o_{p}(n^{-\alpha_{\mu}}),

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\|\widehat{e}\,(X_{S_{k}})-e(X_{S_{k}})\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}=o_{p}(n^{-\alpha_{e}}),

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P}[\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})\,|\,X_{S_{\cap}}]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[{\tau}(X_{S_{k}})\,|\,X_{S_{\cap}}]\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}=o_{p}(n^{-1/4}),

for some
q>2
,
\alpha_{\mu}\geq 0
,
\alpha_{e}\geq 0
such that
\alpha_{\mu}+\alpha_{e}\geq\frac{1}{2}
.

(2)

(strong overlap) The estimated propensity scores
\widehat{e}\,(X_{S_{k}})
are bounded between
[\xi,1-\xi]
for some
0<\xi<1
, almost surely under every
P\in\mathcal{P}
.

(3)

(uniformly bounded moments)
\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}|Y|^{q}<\infty
, and
\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\rm Var}_{P}[Y\mid A=a,X_{S}]\leq C
a.s. for
S=S_{\cap},S_{1},\dots,S_{K}
, for some
C\in(0,\infty)
that does not depend on
P
,

(4)

(finite exponential moments) The moment generating functions of
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{1}};P)\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
and
g(X_{S_{\cap}})
are uniformly bounded in a radius
r>\frac{2q}{q-2}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\|\lambda^{*}(P)\|
around the origin, and the same holds for
\widehat{g}
in the conditional sense:
\{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\exp(r\|\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})\|)\mid\widehat{g}(\cdot)]\}_{P\in\mathcal{P}}
is tight.

Then, the specification-robust estimator
\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}
we compute in
Algorithm

1
satisfies

\widehat{\tau}^{\,\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}(P)\;=\;\mathbb{P}_{n}[\psi^{\,\mathtt{SR}}(D;P)]\;+\;r_{n}(P),

(B.5)

where the remainder term is uniformly asymptotically negligible:
\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\left|r_{n}(P)\right|=o_{p}(n^{-1/2})
, and the influence function is given by

\begin{split}\psi^{\,\mathtt{SR}}(D;P)&:=w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}};P)\,\Bigg[\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu^{*}_{k}\bigl(\tau_{k}^{\,\mathtt{AIPW}}(D;P)-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}(P)\bigr)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(\lambda^{*})^{\top}\bigl(\Delta\tau^{\,\mathtt{AIPW}}(D;P)-g(X_{S_{\cap}};P)\bigr)\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*}(P),P)\Bigg],\end{split}

(B.6)

where
\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu,P):=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}};P)\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}(P)
, with affine weights
\nu^{*}(P)
as defined in
Theorem

3.2
.
Moreover, if the asymptotic variance is uniformly bounded from below, i.e., if
\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\rm Var}_{P}(\psi^{\mathtt{SR}}(D;P))\geq v_{0}
for some constant
v_{0}>0
, then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\,\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|P\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}\left(\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}(P)\right)}{\sqrt{\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}}(P)}}\leq t\right)-\Phi(t)\right|=0,

where the asymptotic variance
\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}}(P)={\rm Var}_{P}(\psi^{\mathtt{SR}}(D;P))
is the same as in
Theorem

3.2
.

Recall that we construct an
(1-\alpha)
-confidence interval for
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}
based on
\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}
as follows.

\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}:=\left[\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}-\frac{z_{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{V}}_{\mathtt{SR}}},\ \widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}+\frac{z_{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{V}}_{\mathtt{SR}}}\right],

(B.7)

where
z_{\alpha}
is the
(1-\alpha)
-th quantile of standard normal distribution and
\widehat{\mathrm{V}}_{\mathtt{SR}}
is an estimator of the asymptotic variance
\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}}(P)
. It is straightforward from
Theorem

B.7
that the above confidence intervals are uniformly asymptotically valid provided that the consistency of the variance estimator is uniform over the class. We state this formally in the following corollary.

Corollary B.8

(Uniform validity of specification-robust confidence intervals)
.

Suppose that
\widehat{\mathrm{V}}_{\mathtt{SR}}
is a uniformly consistent estimator of the asymptotic variance
\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}}(P)
in
Theorem

B.7
, in the sense that

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P(|\widehat{\mathrm{V}}_{\mathtt{SR}}/\mathrm{V}_{\mathtt{SR}}(P)-1|>\varepsilon)\to 0\ \text{ for every }\ \varepsilon>0.

Assume that the class
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\mathtt{SR}}
satisfies the conditions of
Theorem

B.7
. Then, the
(1-\alpha)
confidence interval
\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
for
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}
constructed in (
B.7
) satisfies

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\left|P(\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha})-(1-\alpha)\right|=0.

Appendix C
Main proofs

C.1
Proof of
Lemma

2.2

Proof.

It follows from the construction of the (population) weights
w^{*}
that

\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau(X_{S_{1}})]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau(X_{S_{2}})]=\dots=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau(X_{S_{K}})]=:\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}.

Now
Assumption

2
posits that at least one of the adjustment sets, say
S_{j}
, is valid. Using the ignorability condition
Y(0),Y(1)\perp\!\!\!\!\perp A\mid X_{S_{j}}
, we deduce the following.

\displaystyle\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau(X_{S_{j}})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\mid A=1,X_{S_{j}}]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\mid A=0,X_{S_{j}}]\right)]

(by definition)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y(1)\mid A=1,X_{S_{j}}]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y(0)\mid A=0,X_{S_{j}}]\right)]

(consistency/SUTVA)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})(Y(1)-Y(0))]

(using ignorability and tower property)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{w^{*}P}[Y(1)-Y(0)],

as desired to show.

\square

C.2
Proof of
Lemma

3.1

Proof.

Define
C:=\{\lambda:\psi_{P}(\lambda)\leq 1\}
where
\psi_{P}(\lambda)=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\exp(\lambda^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}}))]
. We first show that
C
is compact. Note that
\psi_{P}
is lower semicontinuous, since for any
\lambda_{n}\to\lambda
, Fatou’s lemma gives

\psi_{P}(\lambda)=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\liminf_{n\to\infty}\exp(\lambda_{n}^{\top}g)\right]\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\psi_{P}(\lambda_{n}).

Therefore
C
is a closed set.
We next show that
C
is convex. Fix
\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}
and
s\in[0,1]
. Convexity of the exponential map
x\mapsto\exp(x)
yields that

\exp\left(((1-s)\lambda_{0}+s\lambda_{1})^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\leq(1-s)\exp(\lambda_{0}^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}}))+s\exp(\lambda_{1}^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}})),

pointwise. Both sides are nonnegative, so taking expectations, we get
\psi_{P}((1-s)\lambda_{0}+s\lambda_{1})\leq(1-s)\psi_{P}(\lambda_{0})+s\psi_{P}(\lambda_{1})
for the possibly extended-real valued function
\psi_{P}
. This proves that
\psi_{P}
is convex. The same argument shows that the effective domain
D=\{\lambda:\psi_{P}(\lambda)<\infty\}
is also convex.

We next prove that
C
is bounded, by contradiction.
If
C
is not bounded, we can take
\lambda_{n}\in C
such that
\|\lambda_{n}\|\to\infty
. Passing to a subsequence, we have

{\lambda_{n}}/{\|\lambda_{n}\|}\to v,\text{ for some $v\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}$ with $\|v\|=1$}.

Fix
t>0
. Since
\|\lambda_{n}\|\to\infty
, we have
t<\|\lambda_{n}\|
for all sufficiently large
n
. Since
C
is convex and contains both
0
and
\lambda_{n}
, we can say that
\frac{t}{\|\lambda_{n}\|}\lambda_{n}\in C
. This combined with
C
being closed implies that
tv\in C
, i.e.,

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\exp(tv^{\top}g)]\leq 1,\text{ for every }t>0.

On the other hand, we know that for some
\varepsilon>0
,
P(v^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}})>\varepsilon)>0
(since otherwise
v^{\top}g\leq 0
a.s. under
P
). As a result,

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\exp(tv^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}}))]\geq\exp(t\varepsilon)\cdot P(v^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}})>\varepsilon)\to\infty,\text{ as }t\to\infty.

This contradiction implies that
C
must be bounded, and thus compact.

We now turn to the main proof. Since
\lambda\mapsto\psi_{P}(\lambda)
is lower semicontinuous, it attains a minimum on
\{\lambda:\psi_{P}(\lambda)\leq 1\}
. Moreover,
0\in C
and
\psi_{P}(0)=1
, so we conclude that the infimum of
\psi_{P}
lies in the closed set
C
. In other words,
\psi_{P}
attains a global minimum at some
\lambda^{*}\in C
. Since
C
is compact,
\lambda^{*}
is finite.

To prove the uniqueness, assume for the sake of contradiction that there are two minimizers
\lambda_{0}\neq\lambda_{1}
. Since
\psi_{P}(0)=1<\infty
, both
\lambda_{0}
and
\lambda_{1}
lie in the effective domain
D=\{\lambda:\psi_{P}(\lambda)<\infty\}
. Invoking condition (i) with
v=\lambda_{0}-\lambda_{1}
and
-v
, we can say that
P(v^{\top}g\neq 0)>0
. The strict convexity of
x\mapsto\exp(x)
implies that

\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{1})^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\exp(\lambda_{0}^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}}))+\exp(\lambda_{1}^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}}))\right),

with strict inequality on the event
\{\lambda_{0}^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}})\neq\lambda_{1}^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}})\}=\{v^{\top}g\neq 0\}
. Since this event has positive probability, we conclude by taking expectations in the above display that

\psi_{P}\left(\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{1})\right)<\frac{1}{2}\left(\psi_{P}(\lambda_{0})+\psi_{P}(\lambda_{1})\right)=\inf_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}}\psi_{P}(\lambda),

which contradicts the minimality of
\lambda_{0}
and
\lambda_{1}
. This proves that the minimizer must be unique.

We now show why condition (i) in
Assumption

3
is also necessary for the existence of a unique, finite minimizer of
\psi_{P}
. Let, if possible, there be
v\neq 0
such that
v^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}})\leq 0
a.s. under
P
. If
P(v^{\top}g<0)>0
, then
t\mapsto\psi_{P}(\lambda+tv)
is strictly decreasing for
t\geq 0
and therefore no finite minimizer exists.
When
v^{\top}g=0
a.s., note that for any minimizer
\lambda^{*}
,
\psi_{P}(\lambda^{*}+tv)
takes the same value for each
t
, showing the minimizer is not unique. We thus conclude that
Assumption

3
(i) is both sufficient and necessary for the existence of a unique, finite minimizer
\lambda^{*}
of the function
\lambda\mapsto\psi_{P}(\lambda)
.

The second conclusion follows from a standard result from information theory
(
Csiszár, 1975
;
Cover and Thomas, 2005
)
; see also
Donsker and Varadhan (1976, Theorem 5.2)
. For completeness, we provide a proof below. Define

w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})=\exp\big(g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\lambda^{*}\big)/\psi_{P}(\lambda^{*}).

(C.1)

By definition,
w^{*}\geq 0
and
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}]=1
.
We now show that
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}\|g\|]<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}g]=0
. By
Assumption

3
(ii),

\lambda^{*}\in\text{int }D
where
D=\{\lambda:\psi_{P}(\lambda)<\infty\}
. Therefore, for any unit vector
u
, there exists
t_{0}>0
sufficiently small such that
\lambda^{*}\pm t_{0}u\in\text{int }D
. For
t\in(0,t_{0}]
, define

f_{t}(X_{S_{\cap}}):=\exp(g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\lambda^{*})\frac{\exp(tu^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}}))-1}{t}.

Convexity of the map
t\mapsto\exp(tu^{\top}g)
implies that
f_{t}
is non-decreasing in
t
. As
t\downarrow 0
,

f_{t}\,\downarrow\,f_{0}:=\exp(g^{\top}\lambda^{*})u^{\top}g,\quad\text{pointwise}.

Since
\lambda^{*}+t_{0}u\in\text{int }D
, we can deduce that
f_{t_{0}}(X_{S_{\cap}})
is integrable. Moreover,
f_{t}\leq f_{t_{0}}
by the monotonicity. Therefore we can apply the monotone convergence theorem for a non-increasing family bounded above by an integrable function to conclude that

\mathbb{E}_{P}[f_{0}]=\lim_{t\,\downarrow\,0}\mathbb{E}_{P}[f_{t}]=\lim_{t\,\downarrow\,0}\frac{\psi_{P}(\lambda^{*}+tu)-\psi_{P}(\lambda^{*})}{t}\geq 0,

where in the last step we used the minimality of
\lambda^{*}
. The above, combined with
f_{0}^{+}\leq f_{t_{0}}^{+}
and
f_{t_{0}}\in L^{1}
implies that
f_{0}\in L^{1}
, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}\|g\|]<\infty
. Applying the same arguments to
-u
yields
\mathbb{E}_{P}[f_{0}]\leq 0
. This gives
\mathbb{E}_{P}[f_{0}]=0
, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}g]=0
.

Thus,
w^{*}
lies in the feasible set for the optimization problem (
3
), given by

\mathcal{S}=\left\{w=w(X_{S_{\cap}})\geq 0,\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[w]=1,\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[w\|g\|]<\infty,\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[wg]=0\right\}.

Fix any
w
in this feasible set, and note that

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{KL}}(wP\|P)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w\log w]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w\log\frac{w}{w^{*}}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{P}[w\log w^{*}]

\displaystyle=D_{\mathrm{KL}}(wP\|w^{*}P)+\mathbb{E}_{P}[wg]^{\top}\lambda^{*}-\log\psi_{P}(\lambda^{*})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{P}[w]

(using (
C.1
))

\displaystyle=D_{\mathrm{KL}}(wP\|w^{*}P)+\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}g]^{\top}\lambda^{*}-\log\psi_{P}(\lambda^{*})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}]

(since both
w,w^{*}\in\mathcal{S}
)

\displaystyle=D_{\mathrm{KL}}(wP\|w^{*}P)+\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}\log w^{*}]

(again using (
C.1
))

\displaystyle=D_{\mathrm{KL}}(wP\|w^{*}P)+D_{\mathrm{KL}}(w^{*}P\|P)

\displaystyle\geq D_{\mathrm{KL}}(w^{*}P\|P),

with equality if and only if
wP=w^{*}P
. This finishes the proof.

\square

C.3
Proof of
Theorem

3.2

Proof.

The proof proceeds in the following steps.
First, we analyze how close the reweighted estimators
\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}
computed in
Algorithm

1
are to their oracle versions; see (
C.2
) for a quantitative statement. The next step is to consider the aggregated estimators and analyze the difference
\sum_{k}\widehat{\nu}_{k}\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\sum_{k}\nu_{k}^{*}\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}
. This difference alone is not asymptotically negligible—we need the bias-correction term to account for first-order bias from estimating the transfer weights. With the additional bias-correction in place, we obtain an asymptotic linear expansion of the (bias-corrected and aggregated) specification-robust estimator, which yields the desired limiting distribution.

Difference of
\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}
and their oracle versions.

Define oracle estimators of the reweighted ATE
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}
as

\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}:=\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}+\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right],\quad k=1,\dots,K,

where
w^{*}
is the population weight function that solves (
3
),
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}
is the ATE for the reweighted population as defined in (
4
), and the AIPW functionals are as defined in
Section

4.2
.
Since
\mathbb{P}_{n}[\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})]=1
, we can deduce using algebra that

\displaystyle\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)-w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\left(\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})-w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\left(\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[{w}^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\left(\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})-w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\left(\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=:\mathbf{I}_{n}+\mathbf{II}_{n}+\mathbf{III}_{n}.

It follows from standard proof techniques for AIPW estimators that
\mathbf{II}_{n}=o_{p}(n^{-1/2})
as well as
\mathbf{III}_{n}=o_{p}(n^{-1/2})
; we delegate these proofs to
Lemma

D.5
. It only remains to tackle the first term.
We apply the tower property to deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{A(Y-\mu_{1}(X_{S_{k}}))}{e(X_{S_{k}})}-\frac{(1-A)(Y-\mu_{0}(X_{S_{k}}))}{1-e(X_{S_{k}})}\;\Big|\;X_{S_{k}}\right]\;\Big|\;X_{S_{\cap}}\right]

(since
S_{\cap}\subseteq S_{k}
)

\displaystyle=0.

(by definition)

We now apply
Lemma

D.15
with
U_{i}=\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)
,
V_{i}=X_{i,S_{\cap}}
,
\widehat{a}=\widehat{w}
and
a=w^{*}
. Note that
{\rm Var}(U_{i}\mid V_{i})\leq 6C/\xi
by
Lemma

D.16
, and
\|\widehat{a}-a\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}=o_{p}(1)
by
Lemma

D.3
. Therefore, we can apply
Lemma

D.15
to conclude that

\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\left(\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})-w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\left(\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]\right)\right]=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}),

which allows us to replace the
\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)
term in
\mathbf{I}_{n}
with
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}_{n}+o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\left(\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})-w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right]+o_{p}(n^{-1/2}).

(C.2)

Difference of aggregated
\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}
and its oracle version.

Recall that
\nu^{*}_{1}:=1-\sum_{k=2}^{K}\nu^{*}_{k}
and

\nu^{*}_{2:K}:=\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\right]\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\tau(X_{S_{1}})-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right].

To quantify how close
\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}-B_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k}\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}
is to its oracle
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu^{*}_{k}\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}
, we begin by writing

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k}\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\widehat{\nu}_{k}-\nu_{k}^{*})\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}(\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*})+\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\widehat{\nu}_{k}-\nu_{k}^{*})(\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}).

(C.3)

For the first term in the above display, use
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}=1
to deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\widehat{\nu}_{k}-\nu_{k}^{*})\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\widehat{\nu}_{k}-\nu_{k}^{*})\,\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=o_{p}(1)O_{p}(n^{-1/2})=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}),

(C.4)

since

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Tower}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\tau(X_{S_{k}})-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:def-tauR}}}{{=}}0.

Next, we show that the last sum in (
C.3
) is asymptotically negligible. To do this, we start from (
C.2
), and apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and
Lemma

D.3
(which shows that
\|\widehat{w}-w^{*}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}=o_{p}(n^{-1/4})
) to deduce that

|\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}|\leq\|\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})-w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}\|\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}+o_{p}(n^{-1/2})=o_{p}(n^{-1/4}),

(C.5)

where
\|\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}=O_{p}(1)
followed from the fact that
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
has finite second moment (since its moment generating function is finite on an open neighborhood of the origin). On the other hand,
Lemma

D.4
shows that
\widehat{\nu}_{k}-\nu_{k}^{*}=o_{p}(n^{-1/4})
—which combined with (
C.5
) yields the following.

\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\widehat{\nu}_{k}-\nu_{k}^{*})(\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*})=o_{p}(n^{-1/4})o_{p}(n^{-1/4})=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}).

(C.6)

Having shown the first and third sums in (
C.3
) are asymptotically negligible (
Eqs.

C.4
and
C.6
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}(\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*})+o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

(C.7)

Denote by
\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu)
the conditional mean of contrasts aggregated using affine weights
\nu
and centered by
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}
, i.e.,

\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu):=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}.

Using this notation and (
C.2
), we can write the right-hand side of (
C.7
) as

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}=\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\left(\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})-w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})\right]+o_{p}(n^{-1/2}).

(C.8)

Using the bias-correction.

In view of the last display, it remains to handle the first-order bias from estimating the transfer weights, which we achieve by using a bias-correction term
B_{n}
such that
\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}+B_{n}
; see
Algorithm

1
for the definition of
B_{n}
. We resume from (
C.8
), and apply
Lemma

D.6
—which gives a Taylor expansion for
(\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})-w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}}))
—to deduce that

\displaystyle\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}

\displaystyle=B_{n}+\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\,\widehat{\lambda}-g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\lambda^{*}\right)\,\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})\right]+o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=:B_{n}+\Delta^{\lambda}_{n}+\Delta^{g}_{n}+o_{p}(n^{-1/2}),

(C.9)

where

\displaystyle\Delta^{\lambda}_{n}

\displaystyle:=(\widehat{\lambda}-\lambda^{*})^{\top}\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})\right],

\displaystyle\Delta^{g}_{n}

\displaystyle:=\widehat{\lambda}^{\,\top}\,\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})-g(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\,\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})\right].

We first show that
\Delta_{n}^{\lambda}
is asymptotically negligible (i.e.,
o_{p}(n^{-1/2})
).
Use
\nu^{*}_{1}=1-\sum_{k=2}^{K}\nu^{*}_{k}
to write

\displaystyle\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu^{*}_{k}\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}-g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\nu^{*}_{2:K}.

On the other hand, the tower property gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\nu^{*}_{2:K}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\tau(X_{S_{1}})-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right]

This implies that

\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}-g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\nu^{*}_{2:K}\right)]=0
,
which we can restate as

\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})]=0.

We combine the above display with the fact that
\widehat{\lambda}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}\lambda^{*}
(from
Lemma

D.2
) to deduce that

\displaystyle\Delta^{\lambda}_{n}

\displaystyle:=(\widehat{\lambda}-\lambda^{*})^{\top}\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})\right]

\displaystyle=o_{p}(1)O_{p}(n^{-1/2})=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}).

(C.10)

It only remains to handle the sum
B_{n}+\Delta^{g}_{n}
in (
C.9
). Recall that
g(X_{S_{\cap}})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\Delta\tau(X)\mid X_{S_{\cap}})]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}(Y,A,X)\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
where

\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}(Y,A,X):=(\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{1}(Y,A,X)-\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{2}(Y,A,X),\,\dots,\,\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{1}(Y,A,X)-\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{K}(Y,A,X)).

We show in
Lemma

D.7
that

B_{n}=\widehat{B}_{n}^{*}+o_{p}(n^{-1/2})
where

\widehat{B}_{n}^{*}=\widehat{\lambda}^{\top}\,\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,(\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}(Y,A,X)-\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}}))\,\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})\right].

(The rationale behind this notation involving both
{\,}\hat{\,}\,
and
*
is that
\widehat{B}_{n}^{*}
combines both estimates and oracle components, and denotes a quantity defined only for the purposes of this proof.)
Using
\widehat{B}_{n}^{*}
as a replacement for
B_{n}
in (
C.9
) and invoking (
C.10
), we can continue from (
C.9
) to write

\displaystyle\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}

\displaystyle=\widehat{B}_{n}^{*}+\Delta^{g}_{n}+o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\widehat{\lambda}^{\,\top}\,\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})\left(\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}(Y,A,X)-g(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\right]+o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\lambda^{*,\top}\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})\left(\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}(Y,A,X)-g(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\right]+o_{p}(n^{-1/2}),

(C.11)

where the last equality is due to the fact that

(\widehat{\lambda}-\lambda^{*})^{\top}\,\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})\left(\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}(Y,A,X)-g(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\right]=o_{p}(1)O_{p}(n^{-1/2})=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}),

which follows from
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}(Y,A,X)\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]=g(X_{S_{\cap}})
and
\widehat{\lambda}-\lambda^{*}=o_{p}(1)
(
Lemma

D.2
).

Asymptotic linear expansion.

We deduce from (
C.11
) that

\displaystyle\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}}_{bc}-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}+(\lambda^{*})^{\top}\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}(Y,A,X)-g(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\,\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})\right]+o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu^{*}_{k}\left(\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right.\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.\left.+\lambda^{*,\top}\left(\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}(Y,A,X)-g(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu^{*}_{k}\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right)\right]+o_{p}(n^{-1/2}),

which gives us the desired result via the classical central limit theorem.

\square

C.4
Proof of
Lemma

4.1

Proof.

Suppose that
w=w(X_{S_{\cap}})
and that
S_{k}
is a valid adjustment set under
P
. Then, since
S_{\cap}\subseteq S_{k}
, the
conditional law of
(Y(0),Y(1),A)
given
X_{S_{k}}
is unchanged under
wP
, and hence
Y(a)\perp\!\!\!\!\perp A\mid X_{S_{k}}
also holds under
wP
.

For the converse, consider
Example

2
: Draw
U_{1},U_{2},X_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
A=\text{ $\mathbf{1}$}{\left\{U_{1}+X_{1}>0\right\}}
,
X_{2}=U_{1}+U_{2}
, and
Y=A\cdot(1+\frac{1}{2}X_{1}-6U_{2})+5U_{2}+\varepsilon_{Y}
where
\varepsilon_{Y}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
independent of
X_{1},U_{1},U_{2}
. In this example,
S_{1}=\{X_{1}\}
is valid, but
S_{2}=\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
is not. To illustrate that an arbitrary reweighting can turn both of these candidate adjustments invalid, consider the following reweighting:

w(X_{1},X_{2})=\sqrt{1-4t}\cdot\exp\left(tX_{2}^{2}\right),\quad 0<t<1/{4}.

Clearly,
w\geq 0
and
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w]=1
. Under
wP
,
(U_{1},U_{2})
remain jointly Gaussian and independent of
X_{1}
, with

{\rm Cov}_{wP}(U_{1},U_{2})=\frac{2t}{1-4t},\quad{\rm Var}_{wP}(U_{1})=\frac{1-2t}{1-4t}.

Consequently,
A=\text{ $\mathbf{1}$}{\left\{U_{1}+X_{1}>0\right\}}
and
U_{2}
are dependent conditional on
X_{1}
. To see this, note that by Gaussian regression,

\mathbb{E}_{wP}\left[U_{2}\mid U_{1},X_{1}\right]=\frac{{\rm Cov}_{wP}\left(U_{1},U_{2}\right)}{{\rm Var}_{wP}\left(U_{1}\right)}U_{1}=\frac{2t}{1-2t}U_{1}.

Since
A=1\left\{U_{1}+X_{1}>0\right\}
, the tower property gives

{\rm Cov}_{wP}\left(U_{2},A\mid X_{1}=x\right)=\frac{2t}{1-2t}{\rm Cov}_{wP}\left(U_{1},1\left\{U_{1}>-x\right\}\right)>0,

where the inequality is strict because

\mathbb{E}_{wP}\left[U_{1}\mid U_{1}>-x\right]>\mathbb{E}_{wP}\left[U_{1}\mid U_{1}\leq-x\right]
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
Finally, since
Y(0)=5U_{2}+\varepsilon_{Y}
, with
\varepsilon_{Y}
independent of
(A,X_{1},U_{2})
under
wP
,

{\rm Cov}_{wP}\left(Y(0),A\mid X_{1}=x\right)=5{\rm Cov}_{wP}\left(U_{2},A\mid X_{1}=x\right)>0.

This implies that
S_{1}
is invalid under
wP
, while
S_{2}
remains invalid because the conditional law of
(A,Y(0),Y(1))
given
(X_{1},X_{2})
is unchanged under the reweighting.

We next show that solving the KL optimization problem with arbitrary weights can produce a noncausal common estimand. Since
\tau(X_{1})=1+\tfrac{1}{2}X_{1}
(see
Section

C.15
for a proof), the difference in contrasts defined in
Assumption

3
is given by

\Delta\tau_{2}(X)=\mathbb{E}_{P}[U_{2}\mid A=1,X_{1},X_{2}]+5\mathbb{E}_{P}[U_{2}\mid A=0,X_{1},X_{2}].

Indeed,
U_{2}\mid X_{2}\sim N(X_{2}/2,1/2)
and, conditional on
(X_{1},X_{2})
,
A=1
is equivalent to
U_{2}<X_{1}+X_{2}
. Thus, writing
z=\sqrt{2}X_{1}+X_{2}/\sqrt{2}
and denoting the standard normal density and cdf by
\varphi
and
\Phi
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[U_{2}\mid A=1,X_{1},X_{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{2}}{2}-\frac{\varphi(z)}{\sqrt{2}\,\Phi(z)},\quad\mathbb{E}_{P}[U_{2}\mid A=0,X_{1},X_{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{2}}{2}+\frac{\varphi(z)}{\sqrt{2}\,\Phi(-z)}.

Both conditional means are strictly increasing in
X_{2}
for fixed
X_{1}
, since
\varphi(z)/\Phi(z)
is decreasing and
\varphi(z)/\Phi(-z)
is increasing. Therefore,
\Delta\tau_{2}(X)
is strictly increasing in
X_{2}
. Moreover, symmetry gives

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\Delta\tau_{2}(X)]=\frac{4}{\sqrt{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\!\left[\frac{\varphi(z)}{\Phi(-z)}\right]>0.

The difference
\Delta\tau_{2}(X)
takes both signs (let
X_{2}\to\pm\infty
with
X_{1}=0
), and has finite exponential moments of every order. Hence the unrestricted version of (3) has the unique KL minimizer

w^{\dagger}(X)=\frac{\exp\{\lambda^{\dagger}\Delta\tau_{2}(X)\}}{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\exp\{\lambda^{\dagger}\Delta\tau_{2}(X)\}]},\qquad\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{\dagger}\Delta\tau_{2}]=0,\qquad\lambda^{\dagger}<0.

For every feasible
w
, the identity
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(wP\|P)=D_{\mathrm{KL}}(wP\|w^{\dagger}P)+D_{\mathrm{KL}}(w^{\dagger}P\|P)
proves optimality and uniqueness. As
X_{2}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp X_{1}
under
P
and
w^{\dagger}
is strictly decreasing in
X_{2}
for fixed
X_{1}
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{\dagger}X_{2}]<0
. Finally,
\mathbb{E}_{P}[U_{2}\mid X_{1},X_{2}]=X_{2}/2
, so

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{w^{\dagger}P}[Y(1)-Y(0)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{\dagger}\tau(X_{1})]-3\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{\dagger}X_{2}]

\displaystyle>\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{\dagger}\tau(X_{1})]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{\dagger}\tau(X_{1},X_{2})],

where the last equality follows from the constraint in (
3
) when solved with arbitrary weights
w=w(X_{1},X_{2})
. Thus, the common weighted contrast is not the ATE of the reweighted population. This completes the proof.

\square

C.5
Proof of
Lemma

4.2

Proof.

We begin by recalling the notation:
\overline{\mathcal{C}}(P):=\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{C}_{k}(P)
, where
\mathcal{C}_{k}(P)
collects all probability distributions of
(Y(0),Y(1),A,X)
that induce the same observed data law as
P
, and imply ignorability for the adjustment set
S_{k}
.
Note that each
\mathcal{C}_{k}(P)
is non-empty: Draw
(A,X)
from their marginal under
P
. If
A=a
, draw
Y(a)
from the observed conditional law of
Y
given
(A=a,X)
, and independently draw
Y(1-a)
from the observed conditional law of
Y
given
\smash{(A=1-a,X_{S_{k}})}
. With
Y=Y(A)
, the resulting
(Y,A,X)
has the same law as under
P
, and satisfies
(Y(0),Y(1))\perp\!\!\!\!\perp A
conditional on
\smash{X_{S_{k}}}
.

For any
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(P)
, the set
S_{k}
satisfies ignorability. Therefore, by the same argument as in the proof of
Lemma

2.2
given in
Section

C.1
, we can say that

\mathbb{E}_{wQ}[Y(1)-Y(0)]=\mathbb{E}_{Q}[w(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\tau(X_{S_{k}})]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\tau(X_{S_{k}})]=:\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP),

where in the middle step we used the fact that
Q
induces the same observed data law as
P
.
Since
\overline{\mathcal{C}}(P)=\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{C}_{k}(P)
and each
\mathcal{C}_{k}(P)
is non-empty, we deduce that

\sup_{Q\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}(P)}\left(a-\mathbb{E}_{wQ}[Y(1)-Y(0)]\right)^{2}\geq\max_{1\leq k\leq K}\left(a-\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP)\right)^{2}.

Finally, taking infimum over
a
and using algebra,

\displaystyle\inf_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\sup_{Q\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}(P)}\left(a-\mathbb{E}_{wQ}[Y(1)-Y(0)]\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\max_{1\leq k\leq K}\left(a-\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\max\left\{\left(a-m\right)^{2},\left(a-M\right)^{2}\right\}\quad\left(m:=\min_{k}\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP),\ M:=\max_{k}\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\min_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{2}\left\{\left(a-m\right)^{2}+\left(a-M\right)^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{\left(a-\frac{m+M}{2}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\left(M-m\right)^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(M-m\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\left(\max_{k}\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP)-\min_{k}\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP)\right)^{2}.

This establishes the desired bound. Next, suppose
w=w(X_{S_{\cap}})\geq 0
,
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w]=1
is such that
\max_{k}\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP)=\min_{k}\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP)=\vartheta
. Then, for each
k
,

\mathbb{E}_{wQ}[Y(1)-Y(0)]=\mathbb{E}_{Q}[w(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\tau(X_{S_{k}})]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\tau(X_{S_{k}})]=\overline{\tau}_{k}(wP)=\vartheta.

(C.12)

Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm arginf}_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\sup_{Q\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}(P)}\left(a-\mathbb{E}_{wQ}[Y(1)-Y(0)]\right)^{2}=\mathop{\rm arginf}_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\left(a-\vartheta\right)^{2}=\vartheta.

Moreover, this unique minimizer
\vartheta
coincides with the ATE of the population
wQ
since (
C.12
) holds, in particular, for the valid adjustment set under
Assumption

2
.

\square

C.6
Proof of
Proposition

4.3

Proof.

All interchanges of differentiation and expectation below are justified by the dominated convergence theorem. Indeed, for
|t|<t_{0}
we have
e_{t}(X_{S_{k}})\in[\xi/2,1-\xi/2]
and
|g_{t}^{\top}\lambda_{t}-g^{\top}\lambda^{*}|\leq|t|(H\|\lambda^{*}\|+H\|h_{\lambda}\|)+(\varepsilon/2)\|g\|
. The integrand
w_{g_{t},\lambda_{t}}H_{t}
and its derivative with respect to
t
are bounded by
e^{(\|\lambda^{*}\|+\varepsilon/2)\|g\|}(1+\|g\|)^{3}(1+|m_{1}|)(1+|Y|+\sum_{k,a}|\mu_{a}(X_{S_{k}})|)
times a constant, and the same bound with
(1+\|g\|)
in place of the last three factors controls the normalizing constant
\mathbb{E}_{P}[e^{g_{t}^{\top}\lambda_{t}}]
and its derivative with respect to
t
. Since
(1+\|g\|)^{3}e^{(\varepsilon/2)\|g\|}\lesssim e^{\varepsilon\|g\|}
, these bounds are integrable under the conditions of
Proposition

4.3
.

We begin by writing down two derivatives, one for the AIPW part and one for the reweighting function.
Note that
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu,e}(Y,A,X)\mid X_{S_{k}}]=\tau(X_{S_{k}})
by the tower property. Along the nuisance path
(\mu_{t},e_{t})
, algebra gives the exact identity

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu_{t},e_{t}}(Y,A,X)\mid X_{S_{k}}]-\tau(X_{S_{k}})=t^{2}h_{e,k}(X_{S_{k}})\left[\frac{h_{\mu,1,k}(X_{S_{k}})}{e_{t}(X_{S_{k}})}+\frac{h_{\mu,0,k}}{1-e_{t}(X_{S_{k}})}\right].

(C.13)

Indeed, suppressing
(X_{S_{k}})
to reduce notational clutter,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu_{t},e_{t}}(Y,A,X)\mid X_{S_{k}}]&=\mu_{1}+th_{\mu,1,k}-\mu_{0}-th_{\mu,0,k}-\frac{te}{e_{t}}h_{\mu,1,k}+\frac{t(1-e)}{1-e_{t}}h_{\mu,0,k}\\
&=\tau+th_{\mu,1,k}\left(1-\frac{e}{e_{t}}\right)-th_{\mu,0,k}\left(1-\frac{1-e}{1-e_{t}}\right),\end{split}

and now use
e_{t}-e=th_{e,k}
to obtain (
C.13
). It follows from (
C.13
) that

\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu_{t},e_{t}}(Y,A,X)\mid X_{S_{k}}\right]\bigg|_{t=0}=0.

(C.14)

Taking conditional expectations, the same conclusion holds conditional on
X_{S_{\cap}}
.

We next differentiate the normalized transfer weight
w_{g,\lambda}
. Suppressing
(X_{S_{\cap}})
to reduce notational clutter, we can write the exponent of
w_{g_{t},\lambda_{t}}
as

g_{t}^{\top}\lambda_{t}=g^{\top}\lambda^{*}+t(h_{g}^{\top}\lambda^{*}+g^{\top}h_{\lambda})+t^{2}h_{g}^{\top}h_{\lambda}.

Therefore, by ordinary differentiation and dominated convergence,

\frac{d}{dt}w_{g_{t},\lambda_{t}}(X_{S_{\cap}})\Big|_{t=0}=w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\Big[\left\{h_{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\lambda^{*}+g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}h_{\lambda}\right\}\\
\qquad\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}\left\{h_{g}^{\top}\lambda^{*}+g^{\top}h_{\lambda}\right\}\right]\Big].

(C.15)

We now turn to the main proof and write the influence function as

\psi(D;\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}},\mu_{t},e_{t},m_{1,t},g_{t},\lambda_{t},\nu_{t}):=w_{g_{t},\lambda_{t}}(X_{S_{\cap}})\cdot H_{t}(D),

where

H_{t}(Y,A,X)=\left(\nu_{t}^{\top}\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu_{t},e_{t}}(Y,A,X)-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right.\\
+\left.\lambda_{t}^{\top}(\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu_{t},e_{t}}(Y,A,X)-g_{t}(X_{S_{\cap}}))\left(m_{1,t}(X_{S_{\cap}})-g_{t}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\nu_{t,2:K}-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\right)\right).

Differentiating using the product rule and dominated convergence, we obtain

\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\psi(D;\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}},\mu_{t},e_{t},m_{1,t},g_{t},\lambda_{t},\nu_{t})\right]\bigg|_{t=0}\\
=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{d}{dt}w_{g_{t},\lambda_{t}}(X_{S_{\cap}})\bigg|_{t=0}H_{0}(D)\right]+\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\frac{d}{dt}H_{t}(D)\bigg|_{t=0}\right].

(C.16)

For the remaining calculation, define

q_{t}(X_{S_{\cap}}):=m_{1,t}(X_{S_{\cap}})-g_{t}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\nu_{t,2:K}-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}.

It follows from tower property and algebra that

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu,e}(Y,A,X)-g(X_{S_{\cap}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]=0,

(C.17)

and therefore

\mathbb{E}_{P}[H_{0}(D)\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\nu^{*,\top}\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu,e}(Y,A,X)-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]=q_{0}(X_{S_{\cap}}).

Moreover, by construction of the transfer weights
w^{*}
and the affine weights
\nu^{*}
,

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})H_{0}(D)\right]=0,\qquad\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})g(X_{S_{\cap}})q_{0}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right]=0.

We now work with the first term on the right hand side of (
C.16
) and use (
C.15
) to write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{d}{dt}w_{g_{t},\lambda_{t}}(X_{S_{\cap}})\bigg|_{t=0}H_{0}(D)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}\{h_{g}^{\top}\lambda^{*}+g^{\top}h_{\lambda}\}\mathbb{E}_{P}[H_{0}(D)\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]\right]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}H_{0}(D)]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}\{h_{g}^{\top}\lambda^{*}+g^{\top}h_{\lambda}\}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[w^{*}\{h_{g}^{\top}\lambda^{*}+g^{\top}h_{\lambda}\}q_{0}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right]\qquad

(since
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}H_{0}(D)]=0
)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}q_{0}h_{g}]^{\top}\lambda^{*},

(C.18)

since
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}gq_{0}]=0
.
It remains to differentiate
H_{t}
. Note that

\left.\frac{d}{dt}q_{t}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right|_{t=0}=h_{m_{1}}(X_{S_{\cap}})-h_{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\nu^{*}_{2:K}-g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}h_{\nu,2:K}.

Therefore, differentiating
H_{t}
using the product rule gives

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{dt}H_{t}(D)\right|_{t=0}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}h_{\nu,k}\{\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu,e}(Y,A,X)-\tau^{R}\}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}\left.\frac{d}{dt}\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu_{t},e_{t}}(Y,A,X)\right|_{t=0}

\displaystyle+h_{\lambda}^{\top}\{\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu,e}(Y,A,X)-g(X_{S_{\cap}})\}q_{0}(X_{S_{\cap}})

\displaystyle+(\lambda^{*})^{\top}\left\{\left.\frac{d}{dt}\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu_{t},e_{t}}(Y,A,X)\right|_{t=0}-h_{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right\}q_{0}(X_{S_{\cap}})

\displaystyle+(\lambda^{*})^{\top}\{\Delta\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu,e}(Y,A,X)-g(X_{S_{\cap}})\}\{h_{m_{1}}-h_{g}^{\top}\nu^{*}_{2:K}-g^{\top}h_{\nu,2:K}\}.

(C.19)

We now take the expectation of (
C.19
) after multiplying by
w^{*}
.

1.

The first sum contributes zero, since
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{\mu,e}(Y,A,X)\mid X_{S_{k}}]=\tau(X_{S_{k}})
,
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}m_{1}]=\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}
and
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}g]=0
.

2.

The second contributes zero as well, by (
C.14
) and tower property.

3.

The second line of (
C.19
) has conditional mean zero, due to (
C.17
).

4.

In the third line of (
C.19
), the derivative of the AIPW contrast has conditional mean zero by (
C.14
); therefore the only contribution from that line is

-(\lambda^{*})^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}h_{g}\,q_{0}].

5.

Finally, the last line of (
C.19
) has conditional mean zero due to (
C.17
).

Consequently,

\mathbb{E}_{P}\!\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left.\frac{d}{dt}H_{t}(D)\right|_{t=0}\right]=-\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}h_{g}\,q_{0}]^{\top}\lambda^{*},

(C.20)

which exactly cancels with (
C.18
), and thus completes the proof.

\square

C.7
Proof of
Lemma

4.4

Proof.

Under
Assumption

2
, there exists one valid adjustment set
S_{j}
. The Donsker-Varadhan inequality gives

\mathbb{E}_{Q}[\varphi]\leq D_{\mathrm{KL}}(Q\,\|\,P)+\log\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[e^{\varphi}\right].

Using this for
Q=w^{*}P
and
\varphi=\pm\,t\,(m_{j}-\overline{\tau}_{j})
, we obtain

\left|\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}-\overline{\tau}\right|=\left|\mathbb{E}_{w^{*}P}\left[m_{j}-\overline{\tau}_{j}\right]\right|\leq\frac{D_{\mathrm{KL}}(w^{*}P\,\|\,P)}{t}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}t.

Optimizing over
t>0
gives us the desired bound.

To show that the bound is sharp, consider the special case where
m_{j}(X_{S_{\cap}})
are jointly Gaussian and
{\rm Var}_{P}(m_{j})=\sigma^{2}
for each
j
. We show in the proof of
Lemma

4.5
that
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}-\overline{\tau}=-\Delta^{\top}\Sigma_{g}^{-1}c_{j}
where
\Delta=\mathbb{E}_{P}[g]
,
\Sigma_{g}={\rm Var}_{P}(g)
and
c_{j}={\rm Cov}(g,m_{j})
where
S_{j}
is a valid adjustment set (exists under
Assumption

2
). Moreover, it follows that

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(w^{*}P\|P)=\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{*,\top}\Sigma_{g}\lambda^{*}=\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{\top}\Sigma_{g}^{-1}\Delta.

Thus,

\frac{|\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}-\overline{\tau}|}{\sigma\sqrt{2D_{\mathrm{KL}}(w^{*}P\|P)}}=\frac{|\Delta^{\top}\Sigma_{g}^{-1}c_{j}|}{\sigma\sqrt{\Delta^{\top}\Sigma_{g}^{-1}\Delta}}.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, this ratio is at most
1
, with equality when
m_{j}-\overline{\tau}_{j}
is perfectly correlated with the tilting direction
\lambda^{*,\top}(g-\Delta)
.

For a more concrete example, consider
X_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
,
A\sim\text{Ber}(1/2)
independent of
X_{1}
, and let
X_{2}
be distributed as
X_{2}\mid A,X_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}((2A-1)(\delta-Z)/2,1)
. Consider potential outcomes
Y(0)=X_{2}
and
Y(1)=X_{1}+X_{2}
, and adjustment sets
S_{1}=\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
and
S_{2}=\{X_{1}\}
with
S_{\cap}=\{X_{1}\}
. It is straightforward to verify that
m_{1}(X_{1})=X_{1}
and
m_{2}(X_{1})=\delta
. These conditional contrasts are jointly Gaussian (allowing degeneracy) and also
\sigma^{2}
-subGaussian. It is also straightforward to derive that the optimal weighting is given by
w^{*}(X_{1})=\exp(\delta X_{1}/\sigma^{2}-\delta^{2}/2\sigma^{2})
, which shifts the marginal of
X_{1}
from
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
to
\mathcal{N}(\delta,\sigma^{2})
. Finally,
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}=\delta
while
\overline{\tau}=0
, and
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(w^{*}P\|P)=\delta^{2}/2\sigma^{2}
. So, in this example,
|\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}-\overline{\tau}|=\sigma\sqrt{2D_{\mathrm{KL}}(w^{*}P\|P)}
.

\square

C.8
Proof of
Lemma

4.5

Proof.

We begin with the special case where the conditional contrasts
m_{k}(X_{S_{\cap}})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
are jointly Gaussian. The proof for this simpler case provides intuition for the more general case.
Using a standard formula, the log-MGF of
g=(m_{1}-m_{2},\dots,m_{1}-m_{K})^{\top}
can be written as

\Psi(\lambda):=\log\mathbb{E}_{P}[\exp(\lambda^{\top}g(X_{S_{\cap}}))]=\Delta^{\top}\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{\top}\Sigma_{g}\lambda,

where
\Delta=\mathbb{E}_{P}[g(X_{S_{\cap}})]
and
\Sigma_{g}={\rm Var}_{P}(g(X_{S_{\cap}}))
. Since
\Sigma_{g}\succ 0
, the unique minimizer of
\Psi(\lambda)
is given by
\lambda^{*}=-\Sigma_{g}^{-1}\Delta
. Now define

U:=(g-\Delta)^{\top}\lambda^{*},\qquad\text{and}\qquad V:=m_{j}-\overline{\tau}_{j}.

Since
m_{j}
are jointly Gaussian, it follows that
(U,V)
is jointly Gaussian with moment generating function
M(s,t)=\exp(\frac{1}{2}(s^{2}\sigma^{2}_{U}+2st\sigma_{UV}+t^{2}\sigma^{2}_{V})).
Differentiating this w.r.t.
t
and setting
t=0
and
s=1
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}_{P}[e^{U}V]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[e^{U}]\cdot{\rm Cov}_{P}(U,V).

Consequently,

\begin{split}\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}-\overline{\tau}=\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}-\overline{\tau}_{j}&=\mathbb{E}_{w^{*}P}\left[m_{j}-\overline{\tau}_{j}\right]\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{e^{U}}{\mathbb{E}_{P}[e^{U}]}V\right]={\rm Cov}_{P}(U,V)=-\Delta^{\top}\Sigma_{g}^{-1}c_{j},\end{split}

as desired to show.

We now turn to the general case. To make the sequence
\{P_{t}\}
explicit in the notation, we use an additional subscript
t
for any quantity that is computed under
P_{t}
, e.g.,

g_{t}=g(X_{S_{\cap}};P_{t}),\qquad\Delta_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}[g_{t}],\qquad\Sigma_{g,t}={\rm Var}_{P_{t}}(g_{t}),\qquad\Psi_{t}(\lambda)=\log\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}[\exp(\lambda^{\top}g_{t})].

We work along a sequence
\{P_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1/2}\,\Delta_{t}\|\to 0
as
t\to\infty
.
Our first step is to make the approximation
\Psi_{t}(\lambda)\approx(\lambda^{\top}\Delta_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{\top}\Sigma_{g,t}\lambda)
precise.
Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\big|\lambda^{\top}\Delta_{t}\big|\leq\big\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\big\|\sqrt{\left\|\Delta_{t}^{\top}\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1}\Delta_{t}\right\|}.

Combining the above with that facts that the MGFs of
\{\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1/2}\,g_{t}\}
are uniformly bounded on a ball of radius
r
around the origin (
r>0
independent of
t
) and
\Delta_{t}^{\top}\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1}\Delta_{t}\to 0
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sup_{t}\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}\left[\exp\left(\pm r\frac{\lambda^{\top}(g_{t}-\Delta_{t})}{\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sup_{t}\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}\left[\exp\left(\pm r\frac{(\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda)^{\top}\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1/2}g_{t}-\lambda^{\top}\Delta_{t}}{\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t}\left\{\sup_{\|\lambda^{\prime}\|\leq r}\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}\left[\exp\left((\lambda^{\prime})^{\top}\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1/2}g_{t}\right)\right]\left(e^{r\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1/2}\Delta_{t}\|}+e^{-r\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1/2}\Delta_{t}\|}\right)\right\}<\infty,

(C.21)

for any
\lambda\neq 0
. Next, using the integral form of Taylor’s theorem,

\left|e^{x}-1-x-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right|\leq\left|\frac{x^{3}}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{2}e^{sx}\,ds\right|\leq\frac{|x|^{3}}{2}e^{|x|}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{2}\,ds=\frac{1}{6}|x|^{3}e^{|x|},

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Using this, we derive that following, for any fixed
\lambda
such that
0<\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|\leq r/2
.

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}\exp(\lambda^{\top}(g_{t}-\Delta_{t}))-1-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{\top}\Sigma_{g,t}\lambda\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{6}\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}\left[\left|\lambda^{\top}(g_{t}-\Delta_{t})\right|^{3}\exp\left(\left|\lambda^{\top}(g_{t}-\Delta_{t})\right|\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|^{3}\,\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}\left[b_{t}^{3}\exp\left(\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|b_{t}\right)\right]\qquad\qquad\qquad\left(b_{t}:=\frac{\left|\lambda^{\top}(g_{t}-\Delta_{t})\right|}{\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{6}\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|^{3}\,\frac{48}{r^{3}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{rb_{t}}{2}+\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|b_{t}\right)\right]

(using that
b^{3}\leq\frac{48}{r^{3}}\exp(\frac{rb}{2})
since
e^{u}\geq\frac{u^{3}}{6}
)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{r^{3}}\,\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|^{3}\,\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}\left[\exp\left(r\frac{\left|\lambda^{\top}(g_{t}-\Delta_{t})\right|}{\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|}\right)\right].

(using
\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|\leq r/2
and the definition of
b_{t}
)

Invoking (
C.21
), we conclude from the last display that

\sup_{t}\left|\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}\left[\exp(\lambda^{\top}(g_{t}-\Delta_{t}))\right]-1-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{\top}\Sigma_{g,t}\lambda\right|\leq C\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|^{3},

(C.22)

for any
\lambda
such that
0<\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|\leq r/2
. We now turn to the log-MGF
\Psi_{t}
, and impose further that
\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|\leq\min\{r/2,1\}
. Note that

\Psi_{t}(\lambda)-\lambda^{\top}\Delta_{t}=\log\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}\left[\exp\left(\lambda^{\top}(g_{t}-\Delta_{t})\right)\right].

It follows by calculus that
0\leq u-\log(1+u)\leq u^{2}/2
for any
u\geq 0
. We apply this inequality with
u=\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}[\exp(\lambda^{\top}(g_{t}-\Delta_{t}))]-1
, which is nonnegative by Jensen’s inequality. The triangle inequality then gives

\displaystyle\left|\Psi_{t}(\lambda)-\lambda^{\top}\Delta_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{\top}\Sigma_{g,t}\lambda\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}\left[\exp(\lambda^{\top}(g_{t}-\Delta_{t}))\right]-1-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{\top}\Sigma_{g,t}\lambda\right|+\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}\left[\exp\left(\lambda^{\top}(g_{t}-\Delta_{t})\right)\right]-1\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|^{3}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}\left[\exp\left(\lambda^{\top}(g_{t}-\Delta_{t})\right)\right]-1\right)^{2}

(using (
C.22
))

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|^{3}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|^{2}+C\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|^{3}\right)^{2}

(by Jensen and (
C.22
))

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|^{3}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}+C\right)^{2}\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|^{4}\leq C_{1}\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|^{3}.

(using
\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|^{3}\leq 1
)

Now choose
5
5

5

The definition of the constants can be traced back:
C_{1}=C+\frac{1}{2}(\frac{1}{2}+C)^{2}
, and
C
is
8r^{-3}
multiplied by two times the last bound in (
C.21
), since we apply
e^{r|b|}\leq e^{rb}+e^{-rb}
.

\rho
such that
0\leq\rho\leq\min\{r/2,1\}
and
C_{1}\rho\leq 1/4
.
Then, we continue from the last display to write the following.

\frac{1}{4}(\lambda^{\top}\Sigma_{g,t}\lambda)\leq\Psi_{t}(\lambda)-\lambda^{\top}\Delta_{t}\leq\frac{3}{4}(\lambda^{\top}\Sigma_{g,t}\lambda),\quad\text{for every}\quad\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|\leq\rho.

(C.23)

Next, we turn to
\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}[(w^{*}_{t}-1)^{2}]
, where
w^{*}_{t}
is the population-level minimizer of the problem (
3
) under
P_{t}
, and denote the corresponding exponential tilt parameter by
\lambda^{*}_{t}
. Note that

\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}[(w^{*}_{t}-1)^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}[(w^{*}_{t})^{2}]-1=\exp(\Psi_{t}(2\lambda^{*}_{t})-2\Psi_{t}(\lambda^{*}_{t}))-1.

(C.24)

Assume for now that
\Delta_{t}\neq 0
. Pick
t
sufficiently large such that
\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1/2}\Delta_{t}\|\leq\rho/16
. Using (
C.23
) and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we conclude that on the boundary
\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|=8\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1/2}\Delta_{t}\|
,

\Psi_{t}(\lambda)\geq-\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|\cdot\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1/2}\Delta_{t}\|+\frac{1}{4}\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|^{2}=8\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1/2}\Delta_{t}\|^{2}>0.

On the other hand,
\Psi_{t}(\lambda^{*}_{t})\leq\Psi(0)=0
. Therefore, using a standard convexity argument,
\lambda^{*}_{t}
lies in the ellipsoid
\{\lambda\,:\,\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda\|<8\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1/2}\Delta_{t}\|\}
. This, in turn, implies that both
\lambda^{*}_{t}
and
2\lambda_{t}^{*}
lie in the ellipsoid where (
C.23
) holds. Applying the upper bound in (
C.23
) at
2\lambda^{*}_{t}
and the lower bound at
\lambda^{*}_{t}
yields

\Psi_{t}(2\lambda^{*}_{t})-2\Psi_{t}(\lambda^{*}_{t})\leq\frac{5}{2}\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{1/2}\lambda^{*}_{t}\|^{2}\leq 160\,\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1/2}\Delta_{t}\|^{2}.

For all sufficiently large
t
, the above bound is at most
1
. Therefore, using
e^{x}-1=\int_{0}^{x}e^{t}\,dt\leq ex
for
0\leq x\leq 1
, we continue from (
C.24
) to write the following.

\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}[(w^{*}_{t}-1)^{2}]\leq 160e\big\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1/2}\Delta_{t}\big\|^{2},

(C.25)

for all sufficiently large
t
. The above inequality trivially holds for
\Delta_{t}=0
.

Finally, suppose that
S_{j,t}
is a valid adjustment set under
P_{t}
(exists by
Assumption

2
). Then, with
m_{j,t}=\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}[\tau(X_{S_{j}};P_{t})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
, we can write

\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}(P_{t})-\overline{\tau}(P_{t})=\mathbb{E}_{P_{t}}[(w^{*}_{t}-1)(m_{j,t}-\overline{\tau}(P_{t}))].

Then, using (
C.25
) and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\left|\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}(P_{t})-\overline{\tau}(P_{t})\right|\leq\sqrt{160e}\big\|\Sigma_{g,t}^{-1/2}\Delta_{t}\big\|{\rm Var}_{P_{t}}^{1/2}(m_{j,t}).

Since
\sup_{t}{\rm Var}_{P_{t}}(m_{j,t})<\infty
, this completes the proof.

\square

C.9
Proof of
Theorem

5.1

Proof.

The proof closely mirrors the proof of
Theorem

3.2
in
Section

C.3
; the key differences are that, the vector of differences-in-contrasts
\Delta\tau(X)\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}
is now replaced with the constraint vector
\delta(X)\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1+d}
as defined in (
18
), and
g(X_{S_{\cap}})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\Delta\tau(X)\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
is replaced with
g(X_{S_{\cap}})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\delta(X)\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1+d}
. Note that here
\lambda^{*},\widehat{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1+d}
,
\nu^{*},\widehat{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1+d}
, and that only the weights
\widehat{\nu}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\nu}_{K}
are used to aggregate the candidate estimators while the bias-correction involves the entire vectors
\widehat{\nu}
and
\widehat{g}
.

Difference between reweighted estimators and their oracle.

Define the oracle estimators of the reweighted ATE
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}
as

\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}:=\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}+\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\left(\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}\right)\right],\quad k=1,\dots,K.

The arguments used in
Section

C.3
to show (
C.8
) apply here verbatim, and yield the following.

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}(\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*})+o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\left(\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})-w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})\right]+o_{p}(n^{-1/2}),

(C.26)

where
\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})
denotes the conditional mean of contrasts aggregated using the affine weights
\nu_{1}^{*},\dots,\nu_{K}^{*}
and centered by
\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}
, i.e.,

\begin{split}\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})&:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{k}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}\\[5.69054pt]
&=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}-g_{1:(K-1)}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\nu_{2:K}^{*}.\end{split}

(C.27)

Now comes a point where this proof deviates from
Section

C.3
: Here we can no longer have
\nu^{*}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,g(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})]=0
(this yields a system of
(K-1+d)
equations in
(K-1)
variables). We handle this issue by modifying the bias-correction so that the quantity
g_{1:(K-1)}^{\top}\nu_{2:K}^{*}
in
\phi
(in (
C.27
)) is replaced with
g^{\top}\nu_{2:(K+d)}^{*}
; see (
C.30
) below.

Extending the oracle part to incorporate additional constraints.

We will use the short-hand
\widehat{\nu}_{f}=\widehat{\nu}_{(K+1):(K+d)}
(and the same for
\nu^{*}
) to remove notational clutter. Observe that the proof of the supporting results

Lemmas

D.5
,
D.1
,
D.4
,
D.2
and
D.3
all go through, with
\widehat{g}
replaced with
\widehat{g}^{0}=(\widehat{g}_{1:(K-1)},f-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f])
. Note also that
\widehat{g}=\widehat{g}^{0}-c_{n}
where
c_{n}=(0_{K-1},\mathbb{P}_{n}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f])=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
. Thus, by triangle inequality,
\|\widehat{g}-g\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}=o_{p}(n^{-1/4})
. As a result, the conclusions we need from
Lemmas

D.5
,
D.1
,
D.4
,
D.2
and
D.3
hold true with
\widehat{g}
as well.

Next, using
\|\widehat{w}-w^{*}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}=o_{p}(n^{-1/4})
from
Lemma

D.3
,
\widehat{\nu}-\nu^{*}=o_{p}(n^{-1/4})
from
Lemma

D.4
, and algebra, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\widehat{w}(f-\mathbb{P}_{n}[f])^{\top}\widehat{\nu}_{f}\right]-\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(f-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f])^{\top}\nu^{*}_{f}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}[(\widehat{w}-w^{*})(f-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f])^{\top}\nu^{*}_{f}]-\mathbb{P}_{n}[w^{*}\nu^{*}_{f}]^{\top}(\mathbb{P}_{n}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f])

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+(\widehat{\nu}_{f}-\nu^{*})^{\top}\mathbb{P}_{n}[w^{*}(f-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f])]+o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}[(\widehat{w}-w^{*})(f-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f])^{\top}\nu^{*}_{f}]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}\nu^{*}_{f}]^{\top}(\mathbb{P}_{n}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f])+o_{p}(n^{-1/2}),

(C.28)

since
\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(f-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f])]=0
by definition. We can now combine (
C.26
) and (
C.28
) to deduce that

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\widehat{w}(f-\mathbb{P}_{n}[f])^{\top}\widehat{\nu}_{f}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R},*}-\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(f-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f])^{\top}\nu^{*}_{f}\right]+\nu^{*,\top}_{f}(\mathbb{P}_{n}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f])\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\left(\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})-w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\phi(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})\right]-\mathbb{P}_{n}[(\widehat{w}-w^{*})(f-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f])^{\top}\nu^{*}_{f}]+o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\left(\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})-w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\widetilde{\phi}(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})\right]+o_{p}(n^{-1/2}),

(C.29)

where

\widetilde{\phi}(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*}):=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}-g(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\nu^{*}_{2:(K+d)}.

(C.30)

The key difference between the above display and (
C.27
) is that the above uses the full constraint vector
g(X_{S_{\cap}})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[(\Delta\tau,f-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f])\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]
, not just its first
K-1
components.

Completing the proof.

With
\widetilde{\phi}(X_{S_{\cap}},\nu^{*})
(as defined in (
C.30
)) replacing
\phi(X_{S_{\cap}},\nu^{*})
, proofs of the supporting results

Lemmas

D.6
and
D.7
also go through. As a result, the same arguments used in the proof of
Theorem

3.2
in
Section

C.3
(precisely, (
C.10
) and (
C.11
)) yield the following.

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\left(\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})-w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\right)\widetilde{\phi}(X_{S_{\cap}};\nu^{*})\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\widehat{\lambda}^{\,\top}(\widehat{\delta}^{\,\mathtt{AIPW}}-\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}}))\left(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}\right]-\widehat{\tau}^{\,\mathtt{R}}_{1}-\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\widehat{\nu}_{2:(K+d)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,{\lambda}^{*,\top}(\delta^{\,\mathtt{AIPW}}-{g}(X_{S_{\cap}}))\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\tau(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}\right]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}-{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\nu_{2:(K+d)}^{*}\right)\right]+o_{p}(n^{-1/2}).

Combining the above display with (
C.29
), we obtain the following
asymptotic linear expansion.

\displaystyle\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR}+}_{bc}-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\nu}_{k}\,\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{\,\mathtt{R}}-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}-\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\widehat{w}(f-\mathbb{P}_{n}[f])^{\top}\widehat{\nu}_{f}\right]

(
Algorithm

B.1
)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,\widehat{\lambda}^{\,\top}(\widehat{\delta}^{\,\mathtt{AIPW}}-\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}}))\left(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\tau}(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}\right]-\widehat{\tau}^{\,\mathtt{R}}_{1}-\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\widehat{\nu}_{2:(K+d)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}\bigg[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{*}\,\tau^{\mathtt{AIPW}}_{k}(Y,A,X)-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\;\;-(w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})-1)(f-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f])^{\top}\nu^{*}_{(K+1):(K+d)}

\displaystyle\qquad\;\;+w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\,{\lambda}^{*,\top}(\delta^{\,\mathtt{AIPW}}-{g}(X_{S_{\cap}}))\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\tau(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}\right]-\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}+}-{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})^{\top}\nu_{2:(K+d)}\right)\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\;\;+o_{p}(n^{-1/2}),

which yields the desired result.

\square

C.10
Proof of
Lemma

A.1

Proof.

The proof essentially follows along the lines of the proof of
Lemma

2.2
.
We deduce from the construction of the (population) weights
w^{*}
that

\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau^{*}(X_{S_{1}})]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau^{*}(X_{S_{2}})]=\dots=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau^{*}(X_{S_{K}})]=:\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau^{*}(X_{S_{j}})]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})(\tau_{j}^{*}+X_{S_{j}}^{\top}\gamma_{j}^{*})]

(by definition)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})(Y(1)-Y(0))]

(by
Assumption

A.1
and the tower property)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{w^{*}P}[Y(1)-Y(0)],

as desired to show.

\square

C.11
Proof of
Lemma

A.2

Proof.

The proof is completely analogous to the proof of
Lemma

3.1
with
\tau(X_{S_{k}})
replaced with
\tau^{*}(X_{S_{k}})
, hence omitted.

\square

C.12
Proof of
Theorem

A.3

Proof.

All the nuisance parameters in
Algorithm

A.1
are finite dimensional. We can therefore view the entire procedure as a single
Z
-estimator and appeal to standard theory, instead of expanding each of its steps separately. We begin by recalling that the specification-robust estimator in this case takes the form

\widehat{\tau}^{\mathtt{SR,lm}}=\widehat{a}_{1}+\widehat{b}_{1}^{\,\top}\mathbb{P}_{n}[\widehat{w}(X_{S_{\cap}})X_{S_{\cap}}].

Observe that

\displaystyle\overline{\tau}^{\mathtt{R}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\tau^{*}(X_{S_{1}})]

(from
Lemma

A.1
)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau^{*}(X_{S_{1}})\mid X_{S_{\cap}}]]

(using the tower property)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})(a_{1}+X_{S_{\cap}}^{\top}b_{1})]

(using
Assumption

A.3
)

\displaystyle=a_{1}+b_{1}^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{P}[w^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})X_{S_{\cap}}].

(C.31)

Next, we set up the entire procedure as a single
Z
-estimation problem.
For generic coefficients
u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{K})
and
v=(v_{1},\dots,v_{K})
, we introduce the notation

g(X_{S_{\cap}};u,v):=\big(u_{1}-u_{2}+X_{S_{\cap}}^{\top}(v_{1}-v_{2}),\dots,u_{1}-u_{K}+X_{S_{\cap}}^{\top}(v_{1}-v_{K})\big),

so that
g^{*}(X_{S_{\cap}})=g(X_{S_{\cap}};a,b)
under
Assumption

A.3
, and
\widehat{g}(X_{S_{\cap}})=g(X_{S_{\cap}};\widehat{a},\widehat{b})
in
Algorithm

A.1
. Let
\theta
collect the regression coefficients
(\delta_{k},\beta_{k},\tau_{k},\gamma_{k})
and
(a_{k},b_{k})
for
k=1,\dots,K
, the tilt parameter
\lambda
, and a scalar
t
(think of the last one as a placeholder for
\tau
). Consider the estimating equations

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[(1,X_{S_{k}}^{\top},A,AX_{S_{k}}^{\top})^{\top}\big(Y-\delta_{k}-X_{S_{k}}^{\top}\beta_{k}-A(\tau_{k}+X_{S_{k}}^{\top}\gamma_{k})\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=0,\qquad k=1,\dots,K,

(C.32)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[(1,X_{S_{\cap}}^{\top})^{\top}\big(\tau_{k}+X_{S_{k}}^{\top}\gamma_{k}-a_{k}-X_{S_{\cap}}^{\top}b_{k}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=0,\qquad k=1,\dots,K,

(C.33)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[\exp\big(g(X_{S_{\cap}};a,b)^{\top}\lambda\big)\,g(X_{S_{\cap}};a,b)\Big]

\displaystyle=0,

(C.34)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[\exp\big(g(X_{S_{\cap}};a,b)^{\top}\lambda\big)\big(a_{1}+X_{S_{\cap}}^{\top}b_{1}-t\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=0,

(C.35)

and denote by
\psi_{\theta}(D)
